Discrete mean estimates and the Landau-Siegel zero


Abstract




Yitang Zhang

Abstract

Let
\chi
be a real primitive character to the modulus
D
. It is proved that

L(1,\chi)\gg(\log D)^{-2022}

where the implied constant is absolute and effectively computable.

In the proof, the lower bound for
L(1,\chi)
is first related to the distribution of zeros of a family of Dirichlet
L
-functions in a certain region, and some results on the gaps between consecutive zeros are derived. Then, by evaluating certain discrete means of the large sieve type, a contradiction can be obtained if
L(1,\chi)
is too small.

\Psi_{1}

L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)
in

L(s,\psi)

\Xi_{11}

\Xi_{14}

\Phi_{1}

\Phi_{2}

\Phi_{3}

Appendix B. Some arithmetic sums

1. Introduction

Let
\chi
be a real primitive character to the modulus
D
. It is known that the Dirichlet
L
-function

L(s,\chi)
has at most one real and simple zero
\tilde{\rho}
satisfying

1-\tilde{\rho}<c_{0}(\log D)^{-1}

where
c_{0}>0
is an absolute constant. Such a zero is called the Landau-Siegel zero.
The typical methods to determine zero-free regions for Dirichlet
L
-functions are unable to
eliminate the Landau-Siegel zero for an intrinsic reason.

The non-vanishing of
L(s,\chi)
near
s=1
is closely related to the lower bound
for the value of
L(s,\chi)
at
s=1
. The well-known
Siegel theorem [19] asserts that, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a positive number
C_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that

L(1,\chi)>C_{1}(\varepsilon)D^{-\varepsilon}.

This implies, for any
\varepsilon>0
, that there exists a positive number
C_{2}(\varepsilon)
such
that

L(\sigma,\chi)\neq 0\qquad\text{if}\qquad\sigma>1-C_{2}(\varepsilon)D^{-\varepsilon}.

However, the process of Siegel’s proof essentially involves certain assumptions, such as
the existence of a real primitive character
\hat{\chi}
(mod
\hat{D}
) with
L(s,\hat{\chi})

vanishing on the interval
[1-\varepsilon,1)
(see [8]),
and the constant
C_{1}(\varepsilon)
relies on the unknown modulus
\hat{D}
necessarily .
This makes the result of Siegel’s theorem entirely ineffective.

It is known (see [9]) that the non-existence of the Landau-Siegel zero implies

[formula]

L(1,\chi)\gg(\log D)^{-1}.

In the case
\chi(-1)=-1
,
Goldfeld [10] and Gross and Zagier [12] proved that

L(1,\chi)\gg D^{-1/2}(\log D)^{1-\varepsilon}

for any
\varepsilon>0
, where the implied constant is effectively computable. This result surpasses the trivial bound

L(1,\chi)\gg D^{-1/2}
and solves Gauss’ class number problem for imaginary quadratic fields.
Granville and Stark [11] proved that the uniform
abc
conjecture for number fields implies (1.1) when
\chi(-1)=-1
.

The main result of this paper is

Theorem 1

If
\chi
is a real primitive character to the modulus
D
, then

L(1,\chi)>c_{1}(\log D)^{-2022}

where
c_{1}>0
is an absolute, effectively computable constant.

As a direct consequence of Theorem 1 we have

Theorem 2

If
\chi
is a real primitive character to the modulus
D
, then

L(\sigma,\chi)\neq 0

for

\sigma>1-c_{2}(\log D)^{-2024}

where
c_{2}>0
is an absolute, effectively computable constant.

It is possible to replace the exponent
-2022
in Theorem 1 by a larger (negative) value if the current arguments are modified, but we will not discuss it in this paper. On the other hand, it seems that the lower bound (1.1) can not be achieved by the present methods.

Acknowledgements.
The basic ideas of the present proof were initially formed during my visit to the Institute for Advanced Study in the spring of 2014. I thank the Institute for Advanced Study for providing me with excellent conditions. I also thank Professor Peter Sarnak for his encouragement.

2. Notation and outline of the proof

Notation and conventions.

Throughout,
t
,
u
,
v
,
z
and
\sigma
denote real variables;
x
and
y
denote positive real variables;
s
and
w
denote the complex variables

s=\sigma+it
and
w=u+iv
;
d
,
h
,
j
,
k
,
l
,
m
,
n
and
r

denote natural numbers, while
p
and
q
denote primes;
the arithmetic functions
\mu(n)
and
\varphi(n)
are defined as usual, while

\tau_{k}(n)
denotes the
k
-fold divisor function. Let
*
denote the Dirichlet convolution of arithmetic functions. Let
e(s)=\exp\{2\pi is\}
. We write

\sum_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{(n,k)=1}

for

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=1\\
(n,k)=1\end{subarray}}^{\infty}

respectively. Let
\int_{(z)}
denote an integration from
z-i\infty
to
z+i\infty
.

We let
\chi
denote a real primitive character to the modulus
D
with
D
greater than a sufficiently large and
effectively computable number. Write

[formula]

\mathcal{L}=\log D.

Let
c
denote a positive, effectively computable and
absolute constant, not necessarily the same at each occurrence. All implied constants, unless specified, are absolute and effectively computable.
In this paper, an error term is usually given in the form that is small enough for our purpose, although shaper estimates are possible.

For any Dirichlet character
\theta
to the modulus
k
, let
\tau(\theta)
denote the Gauss sum

\tau(\theta)=\sum_{a(\bmod\,k)}\theta(a)e(a/k)

(thus we shall not write
\tau
for
\tau_{2}
, the ordinary divisor function).
The expressions

\sum_{\theta(\bmod\,k)},\qquad\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod\,k)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod\,k)}

denote, respectively, a sum over all
{\theta(\bmod\,k)}
, a sum over all non-principal
{\theta(\bmod\,k)}
,
and a sum over all primitive
{\theta(\bmod\,k)}
. Let
\psi_{k}^{0}
denote the principle character
(\bmod\,k)
.

In case
\theta(\bmod\,k)
is a primitive character, the functional equation for
L(s,\theta)
is

[formula]

L(s,\theta)=Z(s,\theta)L(1-s,\bar{\theta}),

where

Z(s,\theta)=\tau(\theta)\pi^{s-1/2}k^{-s}\,\Gamma\bigg(\frac{1-s}{2}\bigg)\Gamma\bigg(\frac{s}{2}\bigg)^{-1}

if
\theta(-1)=1
, and

Z(s,\theta)=-i\tau(\theta)\pi^{s-1/2}k^{-s}\,\Gamma\bigg(\frac{2-s}{2}\bigg)\Gamma\bigg(\frac{1+s}{2}\bigg)^{-1}

if
\theta(-1)=-1
. When
t
is large,
it is convenient to use an asymptotic expression for
Z(s,\theta)
as follows. Let

[formula]

\vartheta(s)=2(2\pi)^{s-1}\Gamma(1-s)\sin(\pi s/2)

(this function is usually written as
\chi(s)
in the literature). It is known that

\pi^{s-1/2}\Gamma\bigg(\frac{1-s}{2}\bigg)\Gamma\bigg(\frac{s}{2}\bigg)^{-1}=\vartheta(s)

and

\pi^{s-1/2}\Gamma\bigg(\frac{2-s}{2}\bigg)\Gamma\bigg(\frac{1+s}{2}\bigg)^{-1}=\vartheta(s)\cot(\pi s/2).

Assume
t>1
. Then

i\cot(\pi s/2)=1+O(e^{-\pi t}).

Thus we have uniformly

[formula]

Z(s,\theta)=\theta(-1)\tau(\theta)k^{-s}\vartheta(s)(1+O(e^{-\pi t})),

and

[formula]

Z(s,\theta)^{-1}=\tau(\bar{\theta})k^{s-1}\vartheta(1-s)(1+O(e^{-\pi t})),

the
O(e^{-\pi t})
term being identically zero in case
\theta(-1)=1
.

Outline of the proof: Initial steps

Our aim is to derive a contradiction from the following

Assumption (A)

L(1,\chi)<\mathcal{L}^{-2022}.

The underlying ideas are inspired by the work of Goldfeld [10], Iwaniec and Sarnak [15] and
Conrey, Ghosh and Gonek [4].
The paper [10] exhibits a relationship between
the lower bound for
L(1,\chi)
and the order of zeros of the function
L_{E}(s)L_{E}(s,\chi)
at
the central point, where
E
is an elliptic curve; the paper [15] exhibits a relationship between the lower
bound for
L(1,\chi)
and the non-vanishing of central values of a family of automorphic
L
-functions;
the paper [4] has definite influence on the idea of reducing the problem to evaluating
certain discrete means that is employed in the present work (the reader is also referred to [3]).

The initial part of this paper consists in relating the lower bound for
L(1,\chi)
to the distribution
of zeros of the function
L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
, with
\psi
belonging to a large
set
\Psi
of primitive characters, in a domain
\Omega
. Thus we introduce

[formula]

P=\exp\{\mathcal{L}^{9}\}.

For notational simplicity we write

p\sim P\quad\text{for}\quad P<p<P(1+\mathcal{L}^{-68}).

Let
\Psi
be the set of all primitive characters
\psi(\bmod\,p)
with
p\sim P
, and let

[formula]

\Omega=\big\{s:\quad|\Re(s-s_{0})|<1/2,\quad|\Im(s-s_{0})|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2\big\}

where

[formula]

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}^{405},\quad s_{0}=\frac{1}{2}+2\pi it_{0}\quad\text{with}\quad t_{0}=\mathcal{L}^{519}.

Throughout, we will assume that
p\sim P
and
\psi
is a primitive characters
(\bmod\,p)
, i.e.,
\psi\in\Psi
.
Note that

[formula]

\mathcal{P}:=\sum_{p\sim P}p=(1+o(1))P^{2}\mathcal{L}^{-77}.

In Section 3 we shall introduce a subset
\Psi_{1}
of
\Psi
and prove

Proposition 2.1.

Let
\Psi_{2}
be the complement of
\Psi_{1}
in
\Psi
. If (A) holds, then

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{2}}1\ll\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-739}.

For
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
, the average gap between consecutive zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
in
\Omega

is

\simeq\frac{2\pi}{\log(Dp^{2}t_{0}^{2})}=\alpha(1+O(\alpha\mathcal{L})).

where

[formula]

\alpha=\frac{\pi}{\log P}.

In Section 4 we shall prove

Proposition 2.2.

Suppose that
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
. Then
the following hold.

(i).
All the zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
in
\Omega
lie on the critical line.

(ii).
All the zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
in
\Omega
are simple.

(iii).
The gap between any consecutive zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
in
\Omega
is of the form

\alpha+O(\alpha^{2}\mathcal{L}).

It should be stressed that the set
\Psi_{1}
is formally defined. In fact, Proposition 2.2 and some other results for
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
are unconditionally derived from the definition of
\Psi_{1}
. However, one is even unable
to determine whether
\Psi_{1}
is empty or not without assuming (A).

The results of Proposition 2.1 and 2.2 are not surprising and they go back to Heath-Brown [15]. One may believe that the assertions (i) and
(ii) of Proposition 2.2 hold for all characters
\psi
in
\Psi
. On the other hand, however,
in comparison with some conjectures on the vertical distribution of zeros of the
Riemann zeta function (see [2] and [17]), one may claim that
the gap assertion (iii) of Proposition 2.2 fails to hold
for most of
\psi
in
\Psi
. Our proof thus consists in deriving
a contradiction from the gap assertion.

Assume
\psi\in\Psi
. Recall that the functional equation for
L(s,\psi)
is given by (2.2) with
\theta=\psi
.
On the upper-half plane, since
Z(s,\psi)
is analytic and non-vanishing, there is an analytic function
Y(s,\psi)
such that

Y(s,\psi)^{2}=Z(s,\psi)^{-1}.

Let

M(s,\psi)=Y(s,\psi)L(s,\psi).

Note that
M(s,\psi)
is defined for
t>0
only, and, for each
\psi
, there are two choices of
M(s,\psi)
up to
\pm
, but the expressions

M(s_{1},\psi)M(s_{2},\psi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{M(s_{1},\psi)}{M(s_{2},\psi)}

are independent of these choices. The functional equation (2.2) gives

M(s,\psi)=Y(s,\psi)^{-1}L(1-s,\bar{\psi}).

Since
|Y(1/2+it,\psi)|=1
, it follows that

|M(1/2+it,\psi)|=|L(1/2+it,\psi)|,

[formula]

M(1/2+it,\psi)\in\mathbf{R},

and, consequently,

[formula]

iM^{\prime}(1/2+it,\psi)\in\mathbf{R}.

The gap assertion (iii) of Proposition 2.2 can be restated as follows. If
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
and
(1/2+i\gamma,1/2+i\gamma^{\prime})
is a pair of consecutive zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)
in
\Omega
with
\gamma^{\prime}>\gamma
, then for some (large) constant
c^{\prime}>0
,

|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma-\alpha|<c^{\prime}\alpha^{2}\mathcal{L}.

Write

[formula]

\beta_{1}=i\alpha(1-5c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}),\qquad\beta_{2}=2i\alpha(1+c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}),\qquad\beta_{3}=3i\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}).

In what follows we assume
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}(\psi)
denote the set of zeros of
L(s,\psi)
in the region

[formula]

|\sigma-1/2|<1/2,\qquad|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}

(this is slightly smaller than
\Omega
).
Assume
\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)
. Note that

M^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)=Y(\rho,\psi)L^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)\neq 0.

Write

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)=\frac{-iM(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)M(\rho+\beta_{2},\psi)M(\rho+\beta_{3},\psi)}{M^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)}.

By (2.11) and (2.12) we have

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\in\mathbf{R}.

It will be proved that

Lemma 2.3.

Suppose
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)
. Then

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\geq 0.

We introduce the smooth weight

[formula]

\omega(s)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\exp\bigg\{\frac{(s-s_{0})^{2}}{4\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}}\bigg\}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}^{400},

which is positive for
\sigma=1/2
. Lemma 2.3 implies that

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)|\mathcal{H}(\rho,\psi)|^{2}\omega(\rho)\geq 0.

for all functions
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
defined on
\Omega
. Thus, in order to derive a contradiction from (A), it suffices to find certain functions
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
and show that , under Assumption (A), (2.16) fails to hold.

A heuristic argument

It is natural to consider the function
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
which is of the form

\sum_{n<P^{\prime}}\frac{f(\log n/\log P^{\prime})\mu\psi(n)}{n^{s}}

for some
P^{\prime}<P
, where
f
is a polynomial with
f(1)=0
. It should be stressed, on assuming (A), that
if
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
is of the above form with
P^{\prime}
slightly smaller than
P
in the logarithmic scale, the sum in (2.16) can be evaluated. This is analogous to the results of Conrey, Iwaniec and Soundararajan [5] and [6]. However, it seems that such a choice of
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
is not good enough for our purpose. On the other hand, the left side of (2.16) could be “close” to zero for certain non-zero functions
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
. For example, one may consider the choice that
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
is a linear combination of
L(s+\beta_{j},\chi\psi)
,
1\leq j\leq 3
. Thus, inspired by the approximate formula for
L(s,\chi\psi)
, some new forms of
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
could be introduced. However, our goal is not to show that (2.16) fails to hold for certain
\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)
directly, instead, a variant of the argument will be adopted.

The crucial part of the proof is to construct functions
H_{1}(s,\psi)
,
H_{2}(s,\psi)
,
J_{1}(s,\psi)
and
J_{2}(s,\psi)
, each of which is of the form

\sum_{n<P^{\prime}}\frac{f(\log n/\log P)\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}

with
\log P^{\prime}/\log P
close to
1/2
, and show that the left side of (2.16) is ”small” if

\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)=H_{1}(s,\psi)+Z(s,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(s,\psi)}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{H}(s,\psi)=J_{1}(s,\psi)+Z(s,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{2}(s,\psi)}

for
\sigma=1/2
. On the other hand, the sum

[formula]

\Xi_{1}^{*}:=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\big(H_{1}(\rho,\psi)\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}+\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}J_{2}(\rho,\psi)\big)\omega(\rho)

is not too small in absolute value.

For
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
and
\rho\in\tilde{Z}(\psi)
, since

\displaystyle H_{1}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}+\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}J_{2}(\rho,\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(H_{1}(\rho,\psi)+Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big)\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}-\big(Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}-J_{2}(\rho,\psi)\big)\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}

and

\big|Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}-J_{2}(\rho,\psi)\big|=\big|J_{1}(\rho,\psi)-Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big|,

it follows that

\displaystyle\big|H_{1}

\displaystyle(\rho,\psi)\overline{J_{1}(\rho,\psi)}+\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}J_{2}(\rho,\psi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big|H_{1}(\rho,\psi)+Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big||J_{1}(\rho,\psi)|+\big|J_{1}(\rho,\psi)-Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big||H_{2}(\rho,\psi)|.

This yields, by Lemma 2.3,

[formula]

|\Xi_{1}^{*}|\leq\Xi_{2}^{*}+\Xi_{3}^{*}

where

[formula]

\Xi_{2}^{*}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\big|H_{1}(\rho,\psi)+Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big||J_{1}(\rho,\psi)|\omega(\rho),

[formula]

\Xi_{3}^{*}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\big|J_{1}(\rho,\psi)-Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{J_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big||H_{2}(\rho,\psi)|\omega(\rho).

Discrete mean estimates

We introduce some parameters and functions as follows. Let

[formula]

P_{1}=P^{0.504},\qquad P_{2}=P^{0.5}T^{-10},\qquad P_{3}=P^{0.498},

[formula]

\beta_{6}=\frac{3i\alpha}{2},\qquad\beta_{7}=\frac{5i\alpha}{2}.

(The definition of
\beta_{4}
and
\beta_{5}
will be given in Section 8.) Write

[formula]

H_{11}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n<P_{1}}\frac{\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\bigg(1-\frac{\log n}{\log P_{1}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{P_{1}}{n}\bigg)^{\beta_{6}},

[formula]

H_{12}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n<P_{2}}\frac{\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\bigg(1-\frac{\log n}{\log P_{2}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{P_{2}}{n}\bigg)^{\beta_{7}},

[formula]

H_{13}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n<P_{3}}\frac{\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\bigg(1-\frac{\log n}{\log P_{3}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{P_{3}}{n}\bigg)^{\beta_{6}}

(these sums are written as
H_{1j}(s,\psi)
rather than
H_{1j}(s,\chi\psi)
). We also introduce the numerical constants

[formula]

\iota_{2}=0.94977-1.38995i,\quad\iota_{3}=-1.00635-0.22789i,\quad\iota_{4}=-0.68738+1.60688i.

We define

[formula]

H_{1}(s,\psi)=H_{11}(s,\psi)+\iota_{2}H_{12}(s,\psi),\quad H_{2}(s,\psi)=\bar{\iota}_{3}H_{13}(s,\psi)+\bar{\iota}_{4}H_{12}(s,\psi).

Let
\tilde{f}(z)
be given by

[formula]

\tilde{f}(z)=\begin{cases}500(z-0.5)&\quad\text{if}\quad 0.5\leq z\leq 0.502\\
500(0.504-z)&\quad\text{if}\quad 0.502\leq z\leq 0.504\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We define

[formula]

J_{1}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\tilde{f}\bigg(\frac{\log n}{\log P}\bigg),

[formula]

J_{2}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\tilde{f}\bigg(\frac{\log n}{\log P}+0.004-\tilde{\alpha}\bigg)\quad\text{with}\quad\tilde{\alpha}=\frac{\log Dt_{0}}{\log P}.

Let

[formula]

\mathfrak{a}=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)^{2}\prod_{q|D}\frac{q}{q+1}.

It can be shown that the assumption (A) implies

\mathfrak{a}\gg 1.

(see Lemma 5.7).

Proposition 2.4.

Assume that (A) holds. Then

|\Xi_{1}^{*}|>5\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}.

Proposition 2.5.

Assume that (A) holds. Then

\Xi_{2}^{*}<2\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}.

Proposition 2.6.

Assume that (A) holds. Then

\Xi_{3}^{*}=o(\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}).

Under Assumption (A), a contradiction is immediately derived from (2.18), Proposition 2.4, 2.5 and 2.6, This proves Theorem 1.

It should be remarked that Proposition 2.5 follows from the inequalities (on assuming (A))

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\big|H_{1}(\rho,\psi)+Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}\big|^{2}\omega(\rho)<0.001\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P},

and

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)|J_{1}(\rho,\psi)|^{2}\omega(\rho)<3000\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P},

by Cauchy’s inequality (and Lemma 2.3). The proof of (2.32) involves some numerical calculations.

We conclude this section by proving Lemma 2.3.

Proof of Lemma 2.3
.   By (2.) we see that
M(\rho+iv,\psi)\neq 0
if
|\beta_{2}|\leq v\leq|\beta_{3}|
. This implies, by the mean-value theorem, that

M(\rho+\beta_{2},\psi)M(\rho+\beta_{3},\psi)>0,

since
M(1/2+it,\psi)
is a real-valued continuous function in
t
. Similarly we have

\frac{M(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{M(\rho+iv,\psi)}>0

if
0<v\leq|\beta_{1}|
. Since

\frac{M(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{iM^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)}=\lim_{v\to 0^{+}}\frac{vM(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{M(\rho+iv,\psi)},

it follows that

\frac{M(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{iM^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)}\geq 0.

This completes the proof.
\Box

Remark.
It is implied in the proof of Lemma 2.3 that

[formula]

\bigg|\frac{L(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)}\bigg|=-i\frac{M(\rho+\beta_{1},\psi)}{M^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)}

for
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
and
\rho\in\tilde{Z}(\psi)
.

3. The set
\Psi_{1}

Let
\nu(n)
and
\upsilon(n)
be given by

\zeta(s)L(s,\chi)=\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)}{n^{s}},\quad\zeta(s)^{-1}L(s,\chi)^{-1}=\sum_{n}\frac{\upsilon(n)}{n^{s}},\quad\sigma>1,

respectively. It is easy to see that

[formula]

|\upsilon(n)|\leq\nu(n)\leq\tau_{2}(n).

Lemma 3.1.

Assume (A) holds. Then

[formula]

\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-2011}.

Proof.
Let

\phi(s)=\zeta(s)^{-2}L(s,\chi)^{-2}\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n^{s}}

which has the Euler product representation

\phi(s)=\prod_{p}\phi_{p}(s)\qquad(\sigma>1).

For
\sigma\geq\sigma_{0}>0
, by checking the cases
\chi(p)=\pm 1
and
\chi(p)=0
respectively, it can be seen that

\phi_{p}(s)=1+O(p^{-2\sigma})\quad\text{if}\quad p\nmid D,

and

\phi_{p}(s)=(1-p^{-s})(1+O(p^{-2\sigma}))\quad\text{if}\quad p|D,

the implied constant depending on
\sigma_{0}
. Thus
\phi(s)
is analytic for
\sigma>1/2
and it satisfies

[formula]

\phi(s)\ll\prod_{p|D}|1-p^{-s}|

for
\sigma\geq\sigma_{1}>1/2
, the implied constant depending on
\sigma_{1}
. The left side of (3.2) is

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}\big(\exp\{-n/P^{2}\}-\exp\{-n/D^{4}\}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\phi(1+s)\zeta(1+s)^{2}L(1+s,\chi)^{2}(P^{2s}-D^{4s})\Gamma(s)\,ds.

Moving the line of integration to the left appropriately and applying standard estimates, we see that the right side is equal to the residue of the integrand plus an acceptable error
O(D^{-c})
. The residue at
s=0
can be written as

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|s|=\alpha^{*}}\phi(1+s)\zeta(1+s)^{2}L(1+s,\chi)^{2}(P^{2s}-D^{4s})\Gamma(s)\,ds

with
\alpha^{*}=\mathcal{L}^{-2024}
. If
|s|=\alpha^{*}
, then

\phi(1+s)=O(1)
by (3.3),

L(1+s,\chi)\ll\mathcal{L}^{-2022}

by (A) and standard estimates, and

\zeta(1+s)\ll\mathcal{L}^{2024},\qquad(P^{2s}-D^{4s})\Gamma(s)\ll\mathcal{L}^{9}.

It follows that the residue at
s=0
is
\ll\mathcal{L}^{-2011}
, whence the result follows.
\Box

Lemma 3.2.

Assume (A) holds. Then we have

\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq D^{8}}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}\tau_{2}(n)^{2}}{n}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-2007}.

Proof.
As the situation is analogous to Lemma 3.1 we give a sketch only. It can be verified that
the function

\phi^{*}(s)=\zeta(s)^{-8}L(s,\chi)^{-8}\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}\tau_{2}(n)^{2}}{n^{s}}

is analytic for
\sigma>1/2
and it satisfies

\phi^{*}(s)\ll\prod_{p|D}|1-p^{-4s}|

for
\sigma\geq\sigma_{1}>1/2
, the implies constant depending on
\sigma_{1}
. Also, one can verify that

\int_{|s|=\alpha^{*}}\phi^{*}(1+s)\zeta(1+s)^{8}L(1+s,\chi)^{8}(D^{8s}-D^{4s})\Gamma(s)\,ds\ll\mathcal{L}^{-2007}.

This completes the proof.
\Box

Lemma 3.3.

For any
s
and any complex numbers
c(n)
we have

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg|\sum_{n\leq P}\frac{c(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\bigg|^{2}\ll\mathcal{P}\sum_{n\leq P}\frac{|c(n)|^{2}}{n^{2\sigma}}

and

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg|\sum_{n\leq P^{2}}\frac{c(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\bigg|^{2}\ll P^{2}\sum_{n\leq P^{2}}\frac{|c(n)|^{2}}{n^{2\sigma}}.

Proof.
The first assertion follows by the orthogonality relation;
the second assertion follows by the large sieve inequality.
\Box

Let

F(s,\psi)=\sum_{n\leq D^{4}}\frac{\nu(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad G(s,\psi)=\sum_{n\leq D^{4}}\frac{\upsilon(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}.

By (3.1) we may write

F(s,\psi)^{20}=\sum_{n\leq D^{80}}\frac{\nu_{20}(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}},\qquad G(s,\psi)^{20}=\sum_{n\leq D^{80}}\frac{\upsilon_{20}(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}

with
|\nu_{20}{n}|\leq\tau_{40}(n)
,
|\upsilon_{20}{n}|\leq\tau_{40}(n)
.
Write

X_{1}(x,\psi)=\sum_{n\leq x}\frac{\nu_{20}(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s_{0}}},\qquad X_{2}(x,\psi)=\sum_{n\leq x}\frac{\upsilon_{20}(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s_{0}}}.

By Cauchy’s inequality and the first assertion of Lemma 3.3 we obtain

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg(|X_{1}(D^{80},\psi)|+|X_{2}(D^{80},\psi)|+\int_{1}^{D^{80}}\frac{|X_{1}(x,\psi)|+|X_{2}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg)^{2}\ll\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{1602}.

Thus we conclude

Lemma 3.4.

The inequality

[formula]

|X_{1}(D^{80},\psi)|+|X_{2}(D^{80},\psi)|+\int_{1}^{D^{80}}\frac{|X_{1}(x,\psi)|+|X_{2}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx<\mathcal{L}^{1171}

holds for all but at most
O(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-740})
characters
\psi
in
\Psi
.

Write

X_{3}(x,\psi)=\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq x}\frac{\nu(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s_{0}}}\quad\text{for}\quad x>D^{4}.

Assume that (A) holds. By Cauchy’s inequality, the second assertion of Lemma 3.2 and Lemma 3.1,

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg(|X_{3}(P^{2},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}\frac{|X_{3}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg)^{2}\ll P^{2}\mathcal{L}^{-1993}\ll\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-1909}.

Thus we conclude

Lemma 3.5

Assume that (A) holds. The inequality

[formula]

|X_{3}(P^{2},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}\frac{|X_{3}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx<\mathcal{L}^{-585}

holds for all but at most
O(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-739})
characters
\psi
in
\Psi
.

Let

\varsigma(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=lm\\
l,m\leq D^{4}\end{subarray}}\nu(l)\upsilon(m).

We have
\varsigma(n)=0
unless
n=1
or
D^{4}<n\leq D^{8}
. Write

X_{4}(x,\psi)=\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq x}\frac{\varsigma(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s_{0}}}

for
x>D^{4}
. It is direct to verify that

|\varsigma(n)|\leq\sum_{n=lm}\nu(l)|\upsilon(m)|\leq\nu(n)\tau_{2}(n).

Assume that (A) holds. By Cauchy’s inequality, the first assertion of Lemma 3.2 and Lemma 3.1,

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg(|X_{4}(D^{8},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{D^{8}}\frac{|X_{4}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg)^{2}\ll\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-2005}.

Thus we conclude

Lemma 3.6.

Assume that (A) holds. The inequality

[formula]

|X_{4}(D^{8},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{D^{8}}\frac{|X_{4}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx<\mathcal{L}^{-633}

holds for all but at most
O(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{-739})
characters
\psi
in
\Psi
.

We are now in a position to give the definition of
\Psi_{1}
:
Let
\Psi_{1}
be the subset of
\Psi

such that
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
if and only if the inequalities (3.4), (3.5) and (3.6) simultaneously hold.

Proposition 2.1 follows from Lemma 3.4, 3.5 and 3.6 immediately.

4. Zeros of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)
in
\Omega

In this section we prove Proposition 2.2. We henceforth assume that
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi_{1}
. This assumption will not be repeated in the statements of Lemma 4.1-4.8.

We begin by proving some consequences of the inequalities (3.4)-(3.6).

Lemma 4.1.

Let

\Omega_{1}=\big\{s:\quad 1/2-(100\mathcal{L})^{-1}\log\mathcal{L}<\sigma<1+(100\mathcal{L})^{-1}\log\mathcal{L},\quad|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+5\big\}.

If
s\in\Omega_{1}
, then

|F(s,\psi)|+|G(s,\psi)|\ll\mathcal{L}^{79}.

Proof.
By the Stieltjes integral we may write

F(s,\psi)^{20}=1+\int_{1}^{D^{80}}\,x^{s_{0}-s}d\{X_{1}(x,\psi)\}.

For
s\in\Omega_{1}
and
1\leq x\leq D^{80}
we have

|x^{s_{0}-s}|\ll\mathcal{L},\qquad\bigg|\frac{d}{dx}\big(x^{s_{0}-s}\big)\bigg|\ll x^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{406}.

Hence, by partial integration,

|F(s,\psi)|^{20}\ll 1+\mathcal{L}^{406}\bigg(|X_{1}(D^{80},\psi)|+\int_{1}^{D^{80}}\frac{|X_{1}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg).

For
G(s,\psi)
an entirely analogous bound is valid. The result now follows by (3.4).
\Box

Lemma 4.2.

If
s\in\Omega_{1}
, then

F(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)=1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-227}).

Proof
.   We have

F(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)-1=\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq D^{8}}\frac{\varsigma(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}=\int_{D^{4}}^{D^{8}}x^{s_{0}-s}\,d\{X_{4}(x,\psi)\}.

Thus, similar to (4.2), by partial integration we obtain

F(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)-1\ll\mathcal{L}^{406}\bigg(|X_{4}(D^{8},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{D^{8}}\frac{|X_{4}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg),

the right side being
O(\mathcal{L}^{-227})
by (3.6).
\Box

Lemma 4.3.

Let

\Omega_{2}=\big\{s:\quad 1/2-\mathcal{L}^{-1}<\sigma<1+\mathcal{L}^{-1},\quad|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+4\big\}.

If
s\in\Omega_{2}
, then

\frac{F^{\prime}}{F}(s,\psi)=O(\mathcal{L}).

Proof
.   Assume
|w|\leq(200\mathcal{L})^{-1}\log\mathcal{L}
, so that
s+w\in\Omega_{1}
. By Lemma 4.1 and 4.2,

\mathcal{L}^{-88}\ll|F(s+w,\psi)|\ll\mathcal{L}^{88}.

Thus the logarithm

\mathfrak{l}(s,w):=\log\frac{F(s+w,\psi)}{F(s,\psi)},

which vanishes at
w=0
, is analytic in
w
, and it satisfies

\Re\{\mathfrak{l}(s,w)\}\ll\log\mathcal{L}.

Since

\frac{F^{\prime}}{F}(s,\psi)=\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\mathfrak{l}(s,w)|_{w=0},

the result follows by Lemma 4 of [15, Chapter 2].
\Box

We proceed to establish an approximate formula for
L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)
. For this purpose we first introduce a weight
g(x)
that will find application at various places. Let

g(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(c)}\frac{x^{w}\omega_{1}(w)}{w}\,dw\qquad(c>0)

with

\omega_{1}(w)=\exp\{w^{2}/(4\mathcal{L}^{30})\}.

We may write

g(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(c)}\bigg(\int_{0}^{x}y^{w-1}\,dy\bigg)\omega_{1}(w)\,dw.

Since

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(c)}\exp\{(\log y)w+w^{2}/(4\mathcal{L}^{30})\}\,dw=\frac{\mathcal{L}^{15}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\{-\mathcal{L}^{30}(\log y)^{2}\},

it follows, by changing the order of integration, that

g(x)=\frac{\mathcal{L}^{15}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{x}\exp\{-\mathcal{L}^{30}(\log y)^{2}\}\,\frac{dy}{y}.

This yields. by substituting
t=\mathcal{L}^{15}\log y
,

[formula]

g(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\mathcal{L}^{15}\log x}\exp\{-t^{2}\}\,dt.

Thus the function
g(x)
is increasing and it satisfies
0<g(x)<1
. Further we have

[formula]

g(x)=1+O(\exp\{-\mathcal{L}^{30}\log^{2}x\})\quad\text{if}\quad x\geq 1

and

[formula]

g(x)=O(\exp\{-\mathcal{L}^{30}\log^{2}x\})\quad\text{if}\quad x\leq 1.

Note that

\chi\psi
is a primitive character
(\bmod Dp)
. Write

\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)=Z(s,\psi)Z(s,\chi\psi),

so that

[formula]

L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)=\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)L(1-s,\bar{\psi})L(1-s,\chi\bar{\psi}).

Recall that
s_{0}=1/2+2\pi it_{0}
. Assume that

|\Re(s-s_{0})|<100,\,\qquad|\Im(s-s_{0})|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+3.

By (2.4) with
\theta=\psi
and
\theta=\chi\psi
we have

\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)=\chi(-1)\tau(\psi)\tau(\chi\psi)(Dp^{2})^{-s}\vartheta(s)^{2}(1+O(e^{-\pi t})).

This yields, by Stirling’s formula,

[formula]

|\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)|=(Dp^{2}t_{0}^{2})^{1/2-\sigma}(1+o(1))

and

[formula]

\frac{\tilde{Z}^{\prime}}{\tilde{Z}}(s,\psi)=-2\log P+O(\mathcal{L}).

Lemma 4.4.

Let

\Omega_{3}=\big\{s:\quad 1/2-\alpha<\sigma<1+\alpha,\quad|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+3\big\}.

If
s\in\Omega_{3}
, then

L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)=F(s,\psi)+\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)F(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(\mathcal{L}^{-179}).

Proof
.   By the residue theorem,

L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\bigg(\int_{(1)}-\int_{(-\sigma-1/2)}\bigg)L(s+w,\psi)L(s+w,\chi\psi)P^{(9/5)w}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}.

By (4.2) and (4.3),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}

\displaystyle L(s+w,\psi)L(s+w,\chi\psi)P^{(9/5)w}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}

\displaystyle=F(s,\psi)+\sum_{D^{4}<n<P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}g\bigg(\frac{P^{9/5}}{n}\bigg)+O(\varepsilon)

where

\varepsilon=\exp\{-c\mathcal{L}^{10}\}.

By (3.5) and partial summation, the second sum on the right side above is

=\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}g\bigg(\frac{P^{9/5}}{x}\bigg)x^{s_{0}-s}\,dX_{3}(x,\psi)\ll\mathcal{L}_{1}\bigg(|X_{3}(P^{2},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}\frac{|X_{3}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg)\ll\mathcal{L}^{-180}.

On the other hand, by the functional equation, for
u=-\sigma-1/2
,

L(s+w,\psi)L(s+w,\chi\psi)=\tilde{Z}(s+w,\psi)\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}.

This sum is split into three sums according to
n\leq D^{4}
,
D^{4}<n\leq P^{2}
and
P^{2}<n
. The proof is therefore reduced to showing that

[formula]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-\sigma-1/2)}

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}(s+w,\psi)F(1-s-w,\bar{\psi})P^{(9/5)w}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)F(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(P^{-c}),

[formula]

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-\sigma-1/2)}\tilde{Z}(s+w,\psi)\bigg(\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)P^{(9/5)w}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-179}

and

[formula]

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-\sigma-1/2)}\tilde{Z}(s+w,\psi)\bigg(\sum_{n>P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)P^{(9/5)w}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}\ll P^{-c}.

To prove (4.7) we move the contour of integration to the vertical segments

\begin{cases}w=10+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|<\mathcal{L}^{20},\\
w=-\sigma-1/2+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|\geq\mathcal{L}^{20},\end{cases}

and to the two connecting horizontal segments

w=u\pm i\mathcal{L}^{20}\quad\text{with}\quad-\sigma-1/2\leq u\leq 10.

By a trivial bound for
\omega_{1}(w)
, (4.5) and the residue theorem we obtain (4.7).

To prove (4.8) we move the contour of integration to the vertical segments

\begin{cases}w=-\alpha+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|<\mathcal{L}^{20},\\
w=-\sigma-1/2+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|\geq\mathcal{L}^{20},\end{cases}

and to the two connecting horizontal segments

w=u\pm i\mathcal{L}^{20}\quad\text{with}\quad-\sigma-1/2\leq u\leq-\alpha.

By a trivial bound for
\omega_{1}(w)
and (4.5) we see that the left side of (4.8) is

\ll P^{1-2\sigma}\int_{-\mathcal{L}^{20}}^{\mathcal{L}^{20}}\bigg|\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s^{*}+iv}}\bigg|\,\frac{dv}{\alpha+iv}+\varepsilon

with
s^{*}=1+\alpha-\bar{s}
. By partial integration,

\sum_{D^{4}<n\leq P^{2}}\frac{\nu(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s^{*}+iv}}=\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}x^{s_{0}-s^{*}-iv}\,dX_{3}(x,\psi)\ll P^{2\sigma-1}\mathcal{L}_{1}\bigg(|X_{3}(P^{2},\psi)|+\int_{D^{4}}^{P^{2}}\frac{|X_{3}(x,\psi)|}{x}\,dx\bigg)

for
|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}
. From this and (3.5) we obtain (4.8).

The estimate (4.9) follows by moving the contour of integration to the vertical segments

\begin{cases}w=-\sigma-10+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|<\mathcal{L}^{20},\\
w=-\sigma-1/2+iv&\quad\text{with}\quad|v|\geq\mathcal{L}^{20},\end{cases}

and to the two connecting horizontal segments

w=u\pm i\mathcal{L}^{20}\quad\text{with}\quad-\sigma-10\leq u\leq-\sigma-1/2,

and applying (4.5) and trivial bounds for
\omega_{1}(w)
and the involved sum.
\Box

In order to prove Proposition 2.2, it is appropriate to deal with the function

\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)=\frac{L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)}{F(s,\psi)}.

By Lemma 4.2,
\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)
is analytic and it has the same zeros as

L(s,\psi)L(s,\psi\chi)
in
\Omega_{1}
. Further, for
s\in\Omega_{1}
, we have

F(s,\psi)^{-1}\ll\mathcal{L}^{79}

by Lemma 4.1 and 4.2. This together with Lemma 4.4 implies that

[formula]

\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)=1+\mathcal{B}(s,\psi)+O(\mathcal{L}^{-100}),

for
s\in\Omega_{3}
, where

\mathcal{B}(s,\psi)=\tilde{Z}(s,\psi)\frac{F(1-s,\bar{\psi})}{F(s,\psi)}.

The proof of Proposition 2.2 is reduced to proving three lemmas as follows.

Lemma 4.5.

If

\frac{1}{2}+\alpha^{2}<\sigma<1,\qquad|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2,

then

A(s,\psi)\neq 0.

Proof.
We discuss in two cases.

Case 1.

1/2+\mathcal{L}^{-1}\leq\sigma<1
.

By Lemma 4.2 and trivial estimation,

\frac{F(1-s,\bar{\psi})}{F(s,\psi)}\ll D^{c}.

Hence, by (4.5),

\mathcal{B}(s,\psi)\ll P^{1/2-\sigma}.

The result now follows by (4.10).

Case 2.

1/2+\alpha^{2}\leq\sigma<1/2+\mathcal{L}^{-1}
.

Assume
1/2\leq\sigma^{\prime}\leq\sigma
. Then both
\sigma^{\prime}+it
and
1-\overline{(\sigma^{\prime}+it)}
lie in
\Omega_{2}
. Hence, by Lemma 4.3 and (4.6),

[formula]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{B}}(\sigma^{\prime}+it,\psi)=

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{Z}^{\prime}}{\tilde{Z}}(\sigma^{\prime}+it,\psi)-\frac{F^{\prime}}{F}(\sigma^{\prime}+it,\psi)-\frac{F^{\prime}}{F}(1-\sigma^{\prime}-it,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\log P+O(\mathcal{L}).

Since

|\mathcal{B}(1/2+it,\psi)|=1
,
it follows that

\log|\mathcal{B}(s,\psi)|=\Re\bigg\{\int_{1/2}^{\sigma}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{B}}(\sigma^{\prime}+it)\,d\sigma\bigg\}<(1/2-\sigma)\log P,

Hence, by (4.10),

|\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)|>1-P^{1/2-\sigma}+O(\mathcal{L}^{-100})\gg\alpha.\qquad\Box

Lemma 4.6.

Suppose
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
is a zero of
\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)
satisfying

\frac{1}{2}\leq\beta<\frac{1}{2}+\alpha^{2},\qquad|\gamma-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2.

Then
\beta=1/2
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\rho,\psi)\neq 0
and
\mathcal{A}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)\neq 0
if
0<|w|<\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
where
c^{\prime}
is a sufficiently constant.

Proof.
It suffices to show that the function
\mathcal{A}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)
has exactly one zero inside the circle

|w|=\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
, counted with multiplicity. By the Rouché theorem, this can be reduced to proving that

|\mathcal{A}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)-(1-P^{-2w})|<|1-P^{-2w}|\quad\text{for}\quad|w|=\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}),

since the function
1-P^{-2w}

has exactly one zero inside this circle which is at
w=0
. In fact, we can prove that

[formula]

\mathcal{A}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)-(1-P^{-2w})\ll\alpha\mathcal{L}

if
|w|<2\alpha
, the implied constant being independent of
c^{\prime}
, and

[formula]

|1-P^{-2w}|>6c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}

if
|w|=\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
.

Assume
|w|<2\alpha
. By (4.10) we have

\mathcal{A}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)-(1-P^{-2w})=\mathcal{B}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)+P^{-2w}+O(\mathcal{L}^{-100}).

Noting that both
s
and
1-s
are in
\Omega_{2}
if
s
lies on the segment connecting
\rho
and
1/2+i\gamma+w
, by (4.11) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{B}(1/2+i\gamma+w,\psi)}{\mathcal{B}(\rho,\psi)}=

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{\int_{\rho}^{1/2+i\gamma+w}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{B}}(s,\psi)\,ds\bigg\}=\exp\big\{-2w\log P+O(\alpha\mathcal{L})\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{-2w}+O(\alpha\mathcal{L}),

Since
\mathcal{B}(\rho,\psi)=-1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-100})
by (4.10), the estimate (4.12) follows.

Now assume
|w|=\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
. Write

w=\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta).

Then

|P^{-2w}|=\exp\big\{-2\pi(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})\cos\theta\big\},\quad\Im\{P^{-2w}\}=-|P^{-2w}|\sin\big\{2\pi(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})\sin\theta\big\};

\text{if}\quad\cos\theta>c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L},\quad\text{then}\quad|P^{-2w}|<1-6c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L};

\text{if}\quad\cos\theta<-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L},\quad\text{then}\quad|P^{-2w}|>1+6c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L};

\text{if}\quad|\cos\theta|\leq c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L},\quad\text{then}\quad|\Im\{P^{-2w}\}|>6c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L},

since

\sqrt{1-(c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})^{2}}\leq|\sin\theta|\leq 1,

so that

|\sin\big\{2\pi(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})\sin\theta\big\}|=2\pi c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L}(1+o(1)).

In either case (4.16) holds.
\Box

Lemma 4.5 and 4.6 together imply the assertions (i) and (ii) of Proposition 2.2. It is also proved that the gap between any distinct zeros of
\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)
in
\Omega
is
>\alpha(1-c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
. To complete the proof of the gap assertion (iii), it now suffices to prove

Lemma 4.7.

Suppose
\rho=1/2+i\gamma
is a zero of
\mathcal{A}(s,\psi)
satisfying
|\gamma-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2
. Then the function
\mathcal{A}(\rho+w,\psi)
has exactly three zeros inside the circle
|w|=\alpha(1+c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
, counted with multiplicity.

Proof.
In a way similar to the proof of Lemma 4.6, it is direct to verify that

|\mathcal{A}(\rho+w,\psi)-(1-P^{-2w})|<|1-P^{-2w}|

if
|w|=\alpha(1+c^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{L})
. Hence, the functions
\mathcal{A}(\rho+w,\psi)
and
1-P^{-2w}

have the same number of zeros inside this circle, while the later has exactly three zeros inside the same circle which are at

w=0
,
w=i\alpha
and
w=-i\alpha
.
\Box

We conclude this section by giving a result which is implied in the proof of Proposition 2.2.

Lemma 4.8.

Assume that
\rho
is a zero of
L(s,\psi)L(s,\chi\psi)
in
\Omega
. Then we have

\tilde{Z}(\rho,\psi)^{-1}=-G(\rho,\psi)F(1-\rho,\bar{\psi})+O(\mathcal{L}^{-100}).

Proof.
It follows from Lemma 4.4 that

F(\rho,\psi)+\tilde{Z}(\rho,\psi)F(1-\rho,\bar{\psi})\ll\mathcal{L}^{-179}.

The result follows by multiplying both sides by
\tilde{Z}(\rho,\psi)^{-1}G(\rho,\psi)
and applying Lemma 4.1.
\Box

5. Some analytic lemmas

Lemma 5.1.

Suppose
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
,
|\sigma-1/2|\leq\alpha
,
|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2
,
u=0
and
|v|<\L^{20}
. Then

[formula]

\frac{Z(s+w,\psi)-Z(s,\psi)(pt_{0})^{-w}}{w}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-114}

[formula]

\frac{Z(s+w,\psi)-Z(s,\psi)(Pt_{0})^{-w}}{w}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-68}

[formula]

\frac{Z(s+w,\chi\psi)-Z(s,\chi\psi)(Dpt_{0})^{-w}}{w}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-114}

and

[formula]

\frac{Z(s+w,\chi\psi)-Z(s,\chi\psi)(DPt_{0})^{-w}}{w}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-68}

Proof.
We have

\frac{Z(s+w,\psi)}{Z(s,\psi)}=\exp\bigg\{\int_{0}^{w}\frac{Z^{\prime}}{Z}(s+w^{\prime},\psi)\,dw^{\prime}\bigg\}.

Assume
|w^{\prime}|\leq|w|
. By (2.6) and the Stirling formula,

\frac{Z^{\prime}}{Z}(s+w^{\prime},\psi)=-\log p+\frac{\vartheta^{\prime}}{\vartheta}(s+w^{\prime})+O(\varepsilon)=-\log(pt/2\pi)+O(1/t_{0}).

Hence

Z(s+w,\psi)=Z(s,\psi)(pt/2\pi)^{-w}+O(|w|/t_{0}).

This yields (5.1) and (5.2) since

pt/2\pi=pt_{0}(1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-114})),\quad pt/2\pi=Pt_{0}(1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-68})).

The proofs of (5.3) and (5.4) are similar.
\Box

Lemma 5.2.

Let
\psi
and
s
be as in Lemma 5.1. Then

\frac{Y(s+\beta_{1},\psi)Y(s+\beta_{2},\psi)Y(s+\beta_{3},\psi)}{Y(s,\psi)}=(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}Z(s,\psi)^{-1}(1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-123})).

Proof.
The left side is

Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\prod_{1\leq j\leq 3}\bigg(\frac{Y(s+\beta_{j},\psi)}{Y(s,\psi)}\bigg).

By (2.6) and the Stirling formula, for
|w|<5\alpha
,

\frac{Y^{\prime}}{Y}(s+w,\psi)=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{Z^{\prime}}{Z}(s+w,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\log(pt_{0})+O(\mathcal{L}^{-114}).

Hence, for
1\leq j\leq 3
,

\frac{Y(s+\beta_{j},\psi)}{Y(s,\psi)}=\exp\bigg(\int_{0}^{\beta_{j}}\frac{Y^{\prime}}{Y}(s+w,\psi)\,dw\bigg)=(pt_{0})^{\beta_{j}/2}(1+O(\mathcal{L}^{-123})).

The result now follows since

\frac{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}}{2}=\beta_{3}.\qquad\Box

Recall that
\vartheta(s)
and
\omega(s)
are given by (2.3) and (2.15) respectively. It is known that

\vartheta(1-s)=2(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s)\cos(\pi s/2).

For
t>1
we have

[formula]

\vartheta(1-s)=\vartheta^{*}(1-s)\big(1+O(e^{-\pi t})\big)

where

\vartheta^{*}(1-s)=(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s)e(-s/4).

Let

[formula]

\Delta_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(3/2)}x^{-s}\vartheta^{*}(1-s)\omega(s)\,ds

and

[formula]

\Delta(x)=\Delta_{1}(x)e(x).

Note that

\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\exp\bigg\{-\bigg(\frac{\pi(x-t_{0})}{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\bigg)^{2}\bigg\}>0.

Lemma 5.3.

If
x\leq t_{0}^{1.02}
, then

[formula]

\Delta(x)=\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)\big(1+O(\alpha)\big)+O(\varepsilon);

if
x>t_{0}^{1.02}
, then

[formula]

\Delta(x)\ll\exp\{-(10^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\log x)^{2}\}+\exp\{-x^{0.99}/\mathcal{L}_{2}\}.

Proof.
By the Mellin transform (see [1], Lemma 2) we have

\Delta_{1}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\{(s_{0}-1)\log y-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}\log^{2}y-2\pi ixy\}\,dy

where the logarithm vanishes at
y=1
. This yields, by substituting
y=e^{u}
,

[formula]

\Delta(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\{s_{0}u-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}u^{2}-2\pi ix(e^{u}-1)\}\,du.

First assume
x\leq t_{0}^{1.02}
. We may write

\exp\{s_{0}w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}-2\pi ix(e^{w}-1)\}=\exp\{2\pi i(t_{0}-x)w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}\}f^{*}(x,w)

with

f^{*}(x,w)=\exp\{(1/2)w-2\pi ix(e^{w}-1-w)\}.

By the relation

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\{2\pi i(t_{0}-x)u-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}u^{2}\}\,du=\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)

and Cauchy’s theorem, the proof of (5.8) is reduced to showing that

[formula]

\int_{L_{j}}\exp\{2\pi i(t_{0}-x)w-\mathcal{L}+2^{2}w^{2}\}\big(f^{*}(x,w)-1\big)\,dw\ll\alpha\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)+\varepsilon

for
1\leq j\leq 5
, where
L_{j}
denote the segments

L_{1}=(-\infty,\,-u^{*}],\quad L_{2}=[-u^{*},\,-u^{*}+iv^{*}],\quad L_{3}=[-u^{*}+iv^{*},\,u^{*}+iv^{*}],

L_{4}=\{u^{*}+iv^{*},\,u^{*}],\quad L_{5}=[u^{*},\,\infty)

with

u^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{5},\qquad v^{*}=\frac{\pi(t_{0}-x)}{\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}}.

If
w\in L_{1}\cup L_{5}
, then

\exp\{2\pi i(t_{0}-x)w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}\}\big(f^{*}(x,w)-1\big)\ll\exp\{-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}u^{2}\}(1+e^{u/2}).

Thus the left side of (5.11) is trivially
O(\varepsilon)
if
j=1,5
. By simple estimates,

\int_{L_{j}}\big|\exp\{2\pi i(t_{0}-x)w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}\}\,dw\big|\ll\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)+\varepsilon

if
j=2,3,4
.
Since
t_{0}^{3.06}/\mathcal{L}_{2}^{4}\ll\alpha
, we have

f^{*}(x,w)-1\ll|w|+x|w|^{2}\ll\alpha

for
w\in L_{2}\cup L_{3}\cup L_{4}
. These estimates together imply (5.11).

Now assume
x>t_{0}^{1.02}
. By Cauchy’s theorem, the proof of (5.9) is reduced to showing that

[formula]

\int_{L_{j}^{\prime}}\exp\{s_{0}w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}-2\pi ix(e^{w}-1)\}\,dw\ll\exp\{-(10^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\log x)^{2}\}+\exp\{-x^{0.99}/\mathcal{L}_{2}\}

where
L^{\prime}_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 3
, denote the segments

L^{\prime}_{1}=\{-\infty,\,-10^{-2}\log x],\quad L^{\prime}_{2}=[-10^{-2}\log x,\,-10^{-2}\log x-i/\mathcal{L}_{2}],

L^{\prime}_{3}=[-10^{-2}\log x-i/\mathcal{L}_{2},\,\infty-i/\mathcal{L}_{2}).

We have

[formula]

\Re\{s_{0}w-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}w^{2}-2\pi ix(e^{w}-1)\}=\frac{1}{2}u-\mathcal{L}_{2}^{2}(u^{2}-v^{2})+2\pi v\bigg(\frac{xe^{u}\sin v}{v}-t_{0}\bigg).

If
w\in L^{\prime}_{1}
, then the right side of (5.13) is

\leq\frac{1}{2}u-(10^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\log x)^{2}.

This yields (5.12) with
j=1
. For
w\in L^{\prime}_{2}\cup L_{3}^{\prime}
we have

xe^{u}\geq x^{0.99}>2t_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad-1/\mathcal{L}_{2}\leq v\leq 0,

so that

2\pi\bigg(\frac{xe^{u}\sin v}{v}-t_{0}\bigg)>x^{0.99}.

If
w\in L_{2}^{\prime}
, then
(\mathcal{L}_{2}u)^{2}=(10^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\log x)^{2}
, so the right side of (5.13) is

\leq-(10^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}\log x)^{2}+O(1).

This yields (5.12) with
j=2
. If
w\in L^{\prime}_{3}
, then
v=-1/\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and the right side of (5.13) is

<1+\frac{1}{2}u-(\mathcal{L}_{2}u)^{2}-x^{0.99}/\mathcal{L}_{2}.

This yields (5.12) with
j=3
.
\Box

As a consequence of Lemma 5.3, the Mellin transform

[formula]

\delta(s):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta(x)x^{s-1}\,dx

is analytic for
\sigma>0
.

Lemma 5.4.

(i).
If
1/2\leq\sigma\leq 2
, then

\delta(s)\ll\mathcal{L}^{c}|s|^{-2}.

(ii).
If
|s-1|<10\alpha
, then

\delta(s)=1+O(\alpha\log\mathcal{L}).

Proof.
(i). Using partial integration twice we obtain

\delta(s)=\frac{1}{s(s+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\Delta^{\prime\prime}(x)x^{s+1}\,ds.

By (5.10) we have

\Delta^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x)=-4\pi^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(e^{u}-1)^{2}\exp\{s_{0}u-B^{2}u^{2}-2\pi ix(e^{u}-1)\}\,du.

Thus some upper bounds for
\Delta^{\prime\prime}(x)
analogous to Lemma 5.3 can be obtained, and (i)
follows.

(ii). Assume
|s-1|<10\alpha
. By Lemma 5.3, on the right side of (5.14), the integral on the part
|x-t_{0}|\geq\mathcal{L}_{1}
contributes
O(\varepsilon)
. For
|x-t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}
we have

x^{s-1}=1+O(\alpha\log\mathcal{L}).

Since

\int_{0}^{\infty}\omega(1/2+2\pi ix)\,dx=1+O(\varepsilon),

(ii) follows.
\Box

Throughout the rest of this paper we assume that (A) holds. This assumption will not be repeated in the statements of the lemmas and propositions in the sequel.

The next two lemmas are weaker forms of the Deuring-Heillbronn Phenomenon.

Lemma 5.5.

The function
L(s,\chi)
has a simple real zero
\tilde{\rho}
such that

[formula]

1-\tilde{\rho}=O(\mathcal{L}^{-2022})

and
L(s,\chi)
has no other zeros in the region

\sigma>1-2\mathcal{L}^{-1},\quad|t|<2D.

Lemma 5.6.

For any primitive character
\theta(\bmod\,r)
with
r<T
and
\theta\neq\chi
,

\sum_{p\sim P}\theta(p)p^{1+it}\ll\mathcal{P}\exp\{-\mathcal{L}^{9/2}\}

if
|t|\leq D
.

Lemma 5.7.

We have

L^{\prime}(1,\chi)\gg\frac{D}{\varphi(D)}.

Proof.
The right side of the equality

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\zeta(1+s)L(1+s,\chi)\frac{D^{4s}\omega_{1}(s)}{s}\,ds=\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)}{n}g\bigg(\frac{D^{4}}{n}\bigg).

is

\gg\sum_{n|D}\frac{1}{n}\gg\frac{D}{\varphi(D)},

since
\nu(n)=1
if
n|D
, while the left side is, by moving the line of integration to the left appropriately and applying (A), equal to
L^{\prime}(1,\chi)+o(1)
.
\Box

Lemma 5.8.

If

\alpha\leq|s-1|\leq 10\alpha,

then

L(s,\chi)=L^{\prime}(1,\chi)(s-1)+O(\alpha_{2})

where

\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{L}^{-15}.

Proof.
This follows from the relation

L(s,\chi)=L(1,\chi)+L^{\prime}(1,\chi)(s-1)+\int_{1}^{s}(s-w)L^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(w,\chi)\,dw,

(A) and a simple bound for
L^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(w,\chi)
.
\Box

Lemma 5.9.

Suppose
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
,
|\sigma-1/2|\leq\alpha
,
|t-2\pi t_{0}|\leq\mathcal{L}_{1}+10
and
|s-\rho|\gg\alpha
for any zero
\rho
of
L(s,\psi)
. Then

\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}\ll\log P.

Proof.
It is known that

[formula]

\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(s^{\prime},\psi)=\sum_{|\rho-s^{\prime}|<1}\frac{1}{s^{\prime}-\rho}+O(1/\alpha)

for
|\Re\{s^{\prime}\}-1/2|\leq\alpha
and
|\Im\{s^{\prime}-2\pi t_{0}\}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+10
, where
\rho
runs through the zeros of
L(s^{\prime},\psi)
.

We can assume
L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)\neq 0
. Suppose
\sigma\geq 1/2
. By (5.16),

-\Re\bigg\{\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(u+it+\beta_{1},\psi)\bigg\}<O(1/\alpha)\quad\text{for}\quad\sigma<u<1/2+\alpha,

so that

\log\frac{|L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)|}{|L(1/2+\alpha+it+\beta_{1},\psi)|}<O(1).

By (9.1) and the condition
|s-\rho|\gg\alpha
for any
\rho
,

\Re\bigg\{\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(u+it,\psi)\bigg\}=O(1/\alpha)\quad\text{for}\quad\sigma<u<1/2+\alpha,

so that

\log\frac{|L(1/2+\alpha+it,\psi)|}{|L(s,\psi)|}<O(1).

Further, by (5.16) and Proposition 2.2 (iii),

\bigg|\frac{L^{\prime}}{L}(1/2+\alpha+it^{\prime},\psi)\bigg|<\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{k<1/al}\frac{1}{k}+O(1/\alpha)<\frac{\log\log P}{\alpha}+O(1/\alpha)

for
t<t^{\prime}<t+|\beta_{1}|
, so that

\log\frac{|L(1/2+\alpha+it+\beta_{1},\psi)|}{|L(1/2+\alpha+it,\psi)|}<\log\log P+O(1).

Combining theses estimates we obtain the result. In the case
\sigma<1/2
the proof is analogous.
\Box

6. Approximate formula for
L(s,\psi)

Write

g^{*}(y)=\begin{cases}g(y)&\quad\text{if}\quad y>1/2,\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let

P_{4}=PT^{-2}t_{0}\quad\text{with}\quad T=\exp\{\mathcal{L}^{1.1}\}.

Lemma 6.1.

Suppose
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
,
|\sigma-1/2|<2\alpha
and
|t-2\pi t_{0}|<\mathcal{L}_{1}+2
. Then

L(s,\psi)=K(s,\psi)+Z(s,\psi)N(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(E_{1}(s,\psi)),

where

K(s,\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{\psi(n)}{n^{s}}\,g^{*}\bigg(\frac{P_{4}}{n}\bigg)

N(1-s,\bar{\psi})=\sum_{n}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s}}\,g^{*}\bigg(\frac{T^{2}}{n}\bigg)

and where

\displaystyle E_{1}(s,\psi)=\mathcal{L}^{-68}\int_{-\mathcal{L}^{20}}^{\mathcal{L}^{20}}\bigg|\sum_{n<T^{3}}\frac{\psi(n)}{n^{s+iv}}\bigg|\omega_{1}(iv)dv+\varepsilon.

Proof.
By (4.3) we have

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}L(s+w,\psi)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}=K(s,\psi)+O(\varepsilon).

The left side above is, by moving the line of integration to
u=-1
, equal to

L(s,\psi)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-1)}L(s+w,\psi)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}.

It therefore suffices to show that

[formula]

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-1)}L(s+w,\psi)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}=-Z(s,\psi)N(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(E_{1}(s,\psi)).

For
u=-1
we have, by the functional equation (2.2) with
\theta=\psi
,

L(s+w,\psi)=Z(s+w,\psi)\bigg(\sum_{n<T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}+\sum_{n\geq T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)

We first show that

[formula]

\int_{(-1)}Z(s+w,\psi)\bigg(\sum_{n\geq T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}\ll\varepsilon.

We move the contour of integration in (6.2) to the vertical segments

u=-1,\quad|v|>\mathcal{L}^{20}

and

u=-\mathcal{L}^{9},\quad|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}

with the horizontal connecting segments

-\mathcal{L}^{9}\leq u\leq-1\quad|v|=\mathcal{L}^{20}.

By a trivial bound for
\omega_{1}(w)
and simple estimates, , the integrals on the segments
u=-1
,
|v|>\mathcal{L}^{20}
contribute
\ll\varepsilon
. On the other hand, in the case
-\mathcal{L}^{9}\leq u\leq-1
,
|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}
, by (4.) we have

Z(s+w,\psi)P_{4}^{w}\ll(2T^{2})^{-u}

and

\sum_{n\geq T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\ll T^{3u+1/2},

whence (6.2) follows. The proof of (6.1) is therefore reduced to showing that

[formula]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-1-i\mathcal{L}^{20}}^{-1+i\mathcal{L}^{20}}Z(s+w,\psi)

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{n<T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-Z(s,\psi)N(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(E_{1}(s,\psi)).

Note that
Pt_{0}/P_{4}=T^{2}
. The left side of (6.3) is equal to

I^{\prime}+I{{}^{\prime\prime}}

with

[formula]

I^{\prime}=Z(s,\psi)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-1-i\mathcal{L}^{20}}^{-1+i\mathcal{L}^{20}}\bigg(\sum_{n<T^{3}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)\frac{T^{-2w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w},

[formula]

I^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(-1)}\big(Z(s+w,\psi)-Z(s,\psi)(Pt_{0})^{-w}\big)\bigg(\sum_{n<T^{2}}\frac{\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s-w}}\bigg)\frac{P_{4}^{w}\omega_{1}(w)\,dw}{w}.

Replacing the segment
u=-1
,
|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}
by
u=-1
and using the change of variable
w\to-w
, we obtain

I^{\prime}=-Z(s,\psi)N(1-s,\bar{\psi})+O(\varepsilon).

On the other hand, moving the segment
u=-1
,
|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}
to
u=0
,
|v|\leq\mathcal{L}^{20}
and applying Lemma 5.1, we find the
the right side of (6.5) is
\ll E_{1}(s,\psi)
.
\Box

7. Mean-value formula I

Let
\mathcal{N}(d)
denote the set of positive integers such that
h\in\mathcal{N}(d)
if and only if every prime factor of
h
divides
d
(note that
1\in\mathcal{N}(d)
for every
d
and
\mathcal{N}(1)=\{1\}
). Assume
1\leq j\leq 3
in what follows. Write

\tilde{\kappa}(d;m,s)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\mathcal{N}(d)\\
(h,m)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\kappa(dh)}{h^{s}},

and

\lambda(m,s)=\prod_{q|m}\frac{(1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{2}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{3}})}{1-q^{-s}}.

For notational simplicity we write

\tilde{\kappa}(d;m,1-\beta_{j})=\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d;m),\quad\lambda(m,1-\beta_{j})=\lambda_{0j}(m).

Let

\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)=\tilde{\lambda}_{0j}(n,dr)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=d_{1}k\\
(k,r)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1};drk)\mu(k)k^{1-\beta_{j}}}{\varphi(k)}

with

\tilde{\lambda}_{0j}(n,dr)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|n\\
(q,dr)=1\end{subarray}}\lambda_{0j}(q),

For
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
write

[formula]

\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)=-i(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)L(s+\beta_{2},\psi)L(s+\beta_{3},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}.

Let
\mathbb{a}=\{a(n)\}
denote a sequence of complex numbers satisfying

[formula]

a(n)\ll 1,\quad a(n)=0\quad\text{if}\quad n\geq PT^{-2}.

Write

\bar{\mathbb{a}}=\{\bar{a}(n)\},\quad\bar{a}(n)=\overline{a(n)},

A(\mathbb{a};s,\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{a(n)\psi(n))}{n^{s}},\quad A(\mathbb{a};1-s,\bar{\psi})=\sum_{n}\frac{a(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s}}.

Let
\mathcal{J}(z)
denote the segment
[s_{0}+z-i\mathcal{L}_{1},\,s_{0}+z+i\mathcal{L}_{1}]
.

The goal of this section is to prove

Proposition 7.1.

Assume
\mathbb{a}_{1}
and
\mathbb{a}_{2}
satisfy (7.2).
We have

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2}):

\displaystyle=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+2S_{2}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+\frac{3}{2}S_{3}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})\bigg)\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle+O(E(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2}))+o(\mathcal{P}),

where

\mathcal{P}=\sum_{p\sim P}p,

S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=\sum_{d}\sum_{r}\frac{|\mu(r)|\lambda_{0j}(dr)}{dr\varphi(r)}\bigg(\sum_{m}\frac{a_{1}(drm)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{n}\frac{a_{2}(drn)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)}{n}\bigg),

and

E(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{L}^{2}\sum_{1\leq j\leq 3}|S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})|.

In this and the next two sections we assume that
1\leq j\leq 3
.

Proof of Proposition 7.1: Initial steps

For notational simplicity we write
\Theta_{1}
for
\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})
. Note that for any
\psi\in\Psi
,
\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)
is analytic if
\sigma>1
and
|t-2\pi t_{0}|\leq\mathcal{L}_{1}
. We need to show that the sum over
\Psi_{1}
in the expression for
\Theta_{1}
can be extend to the sum over
\Psi
with an acceptable error. Namely we prove

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{2}}\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds=o(\mathcal{P}).

Here Proposition 2.1 is crucial.

Let
\kappa(n)
be given by

\sum_{n}\frac{\kappa(n)}{n^{s}}=\frac{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})\zeta(s+\beta_{2})\zeta(s+\beta_{3})}{\zeta(s)},

and regard
a_{1}
as an arithmetic function. For
\sigma>1

\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)L(s+\beta_{2},\psi)L(s+\beta_{3},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)=\sum_{m}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}.

Thus, for
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
and
s\in\mathcal{J}(1)
,

\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)=-i(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\sum_{m}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}.

The sum over
m
is split into two sums according to
m<P^{2}
and
m\geq P^{2}
. To handle the second one we appeal to the trivial bounds

\quad\sum_{m\geq P^{2}}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\ll P^{2(1-\sigma)}\mathcal{L}^{c},\quad A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\ll(PT^{-2})^{\sigma}

for
\sigma\geq 3/2
, and

Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\ll(pt_{0})^{\sigma-1/2}

for
3/2\leq\sigma\leq\mathcal{L}^{9}
,
|t-2\pi t_{0}|\leq\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Thus, moving the segment to
\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{L}^{9})
and applying the simple estimate

[formula]

\int_{\mathcal{J}(z)}|\omega(s)\,ds|\ll 1,\quad|z|\leq\mathcal{L}^{9},

we obtain

\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\bigg(\sum_{m\geq P^{2}}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\bigg)A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds\ll\varepsilon.

To handle the sum over
m<P^{2}
we move the path of integration to
\mathcal{J}(0)
. Hence

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})(s,\psi)\omega(s)\,ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{J}(0)}\bigg|\sum_{m<P^{2}}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\bigg||A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds|+O(\varepsilon).

By (7.4), the proof of (7.3) is reduced to showing that

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{2}}\bigg|\sum_{m<P^{2}}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\bigg||A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})|=o(\mathcal{P}),\quad\sigma=1/2.

Note that
(\kappa*a_{1})(m)=O(\tau_{5}(m))
. The left side above is, by Cauchy’s inequality,

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\bigg|\sum_{m<P^{2}}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\bigg|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}|A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})|^{4}\bigg)^{1/4}\bigg(\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{2}}1\bigg)^{1/4}

\displaystyle\ll\bigg(P^{2}\sum_{m<P^{2}}\frac{\tau_{5}(m)^{2}}{m}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(P^{2}\sum_{m<P^{2}}\frac{\tau_{2}(m)^{2}}{m}\bigg)^{1/4}\bigg(\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{2}}1\bigg)^{1/4}.

This yields (7.5) by Proposition 2.1 and (2.9).

By (7.3) we may write

[formula]

\Theta_{1}=-i\sum_{p\sim P}(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}I_{1}(\psi)+o(\mathcal{P})

where

I_{1}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}Z(s,\psi)^{-1}\bigg(\sum_{m}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}\bigg)A(\mathbb{a}_{2};1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds.

Assume
\psi(\bmod\,p)\in\Psi
. By (2.4), in the integral
I_{1}(\psi)
, the factor
Z(s,\psi)^{-1}
can be replaced by

\tau(\bar{\psi})p^{s-1}\vartheta^{*}(1-s),

and then, by a trivial bound for
\vartheta^{*}(1-s)\omega(s)
on
\sigma=3/2
, the segment
\mathcal{J}(1)
can be replaced by the line
\sigma=3/2
, with negligible errors. Thus, integration term-by–term gives

I_{1}(\psi)=\frac{\tau(\bar{\psi})}{p}\sum_{m}\,\sum_{n}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)a_{2}(n)\psi(m)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n}\Delta_{1}\bigg(\frac{m}{pn}\bigg)+O(\varepsilon).

This yields

\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}I_{1}(\psi)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{m}\,\sum_{n}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(m)a_{2}(n)}{n}\Delta_{1}\bigg(\frac{m}{pn}\bigg)\bigg(\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}\tau(\bar{\psi})\psi(m)\bar{\psi}(n)\bigg)+O(\varepsilon).

Note that
(n,p)=1
if
n<PT^{-2}
. On substituting
d=(m,n)
,
m=dl
,
n=dk
, we find that the main term on the right side above is

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{d}\,\frac{1}{d}\sum_{l}\,\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)a_{2}(dk)}{k}\bigg(\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}\tau(\bar{\psi})\psi(l)\bar{\psi}(k)\bigg)\Delta_{1}\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg).

Since
\tau(\psi_{p}^{0})=-1
, for
(kl,p)=1
we have

\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod p)}\tau(\bar{\psi})\psi(l)\bar{\psi}(k)=pe\bigg(\frac{l\bar{k}}{p}\bigg)+O(1)

with
\bar{k}k\equiv 1(\bmod\,p)
. Hence, by Lemma 5.4,

[formula]

\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}I(\psi)=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{(l,p)=1}\,\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)b(dk)}{k}e\bigg(\frac{l\bar{k}}{p}\bigg)\Delta_{1}\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)+O(PT^{-c}).

By trivial estimation, this remains valid if the constraint
(l,p)=1
is removed. Further, by the relation

\frac{\bar{k}}{p}\equiv-\frac{\bar{p}}{k}+\frac{1}{pk}\qquad(\bmod\,1)

we have

e\bigg(\frac{l\bar{k}}{p}\bigg)\Delta_{1}\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)=e\bigg(-\frac{l\bar{p}}{k}\bigg)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)

Thus the right side of (7.7) is

[formula]

\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{l}\,\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)b(dk)}{k}e\bigg(-\frac{l\bar{p}}{k}\bigg)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)+O(PT^{-c}).

For
(l,k)=1
we have

e\bigg(\frac{-l\bar{p}}{k}\bigg)=\frac{\mu(k)}{\varphi(k)}+\frac{1}{\varphi(k)}\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod k)}\tau(\bar{\theta})\theta(l)\bar{\theta}(-p).

Inserting this into (7.8) we deduce that

[formula]

\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}I(\psi)=\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)+\mathcal{T}_{12}(p)+O(PT^{-c})

where

\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{l}\,\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)a_{2}(dk)\mu(k)}{k\varphi(k)}\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)

and

\mathcal{T}_{12}(p)=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{l}\,\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)a_{2}(dk)}{k\varphi(k)}\bigg(\,\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod k)}\tau(\bar{\theta})\theta(l)\bar{\theta}(-p)\bigg)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg).

Note that
(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}=-1+O(\alpha_{1})
. By (7.6) and (7.9), the proof of Proposition 7.1 is reduced to showing that

[formula]

\displaystyle i\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)=

\displaystyle\frac{p}{\alpha}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}S_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+2S_{2}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+\frac{3}{2}S_{3}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})\bigg)

\displaystyle+O\bigg(P\mathcal{L}^{2}\sum_{1\leq j\leq 3}|S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})|\bigg)+o(P)

for
p\sim P
, and

[formula]

\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\mathcal{T}_{12}(p)=o(\mathcal{P}).

Proof of Proposition 7.1: The error term

In this subsection we prove (7.11).

Changing the order of summation gives

[formula]

\displaystyle\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\mathcal{T}_{12}(p)=

\displaystyle\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k}\,\frac{a_{2}(dk)}{k\varphi(k)}\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod k)}\tau(\bar{\theta})\sum_{l}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\theta(l)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\bar{\theta}(-p)\,\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg).

If
k=hr
,
r>1
and
\theta(\bmod\,k)
is induced by a primitive character
\theta^{*}(\bmod\,r)
, then
|\tau(\bar{\theta})|\leq r^{1/2}
. Thus, the inner sum over the non-principal
\theta(\bmod\,k)
above is

\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}hr=k\\
r>1\end{subarray}}r^{1/2}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}|\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)|,

where

\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)=\sum_{(l,h)=1}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\theta(l)\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\bar{\theta}(p)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{phr}\bigg).

Inserting this into (7.12) we obtain

[formula]

\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\mathcal{T}_{12}(p)\ll\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\{d,h,r\}}\frac{1}{dh\varphi(hr)\sqrt{r}}\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}|\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)|,

where
\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\{d,h,r\}}
denotes a sum over the triples
\{d,h,r\}
satisfying
dhr<P_{1}
and
r>1
. By Lemma 5.3, for
dhr<P_{1}
, the terms in
\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)
with
l>P^{2}
make a negligible error. Also, by the Mellin transform and Lemma 5.4 (i),

[formula]

\sum_{p\sim P}p^{\beta_{3}}\bar{\theta}(p)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{phr}\bigg)\ll\mathcal{L}^{c}hrl^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg|\sum_{p\sim P}p^{1+it+\beta_{3}}\bar{\theta}(p)\bigg|\frac{dt}{1+t^{2}}

for
l\leq P^{2}
.

Assume
1<r<D
and
\theta
is a primitive character
(\bmod\,r)
. By Lemma 5.6, the right side of (7.14) is

\ll\frac{hr}{l}P^{2}D^{-c}.

Hence

\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)\ll\tau_{5}(d)hrP^{2}D^{-c}.

Thus, on the right side of (7.13), the total contribution from the terms with
1<r<D
is
O(P^{2}D^{-c})
. The proof of
(7.11) is therefore reduced to showing that

[formula]

\frac{1}{R^{3/2}}\sum_{R\leq r<2R}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}|\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)|\ll\tau_{5}(d)hP^{2}D^{-c}

for

dh<P_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad D\leq R<(dh)^{-1}P_{1}

which are henceforth assumed .

Let
\mathfrak{I}(y)
denote the interval

[(1/3)Pt_{0}y,\,4Pt_{0}y].

By Lemma 5.1, for
R\leq r<2R
, the terms with

l\notin\mathfrak{I}(Rh)

in
\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)
make a negligible contribution. Thus, it suffices to prove (7.15) with
\mathcal{S}(r,h,d;\theta)
replaced by

\mathcal{S}^{*}(R,r,h,d;\theta):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathfrak{I}(Rh)\\
(l,h)=1\end{subarray}}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\theta(l)\sum_{p\simeq P}\bar{\theta}(p)p^{\beta_{3}}\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{phr}\bigg).

By the Mellin transform and Lemma 5.4 (i),

\mathcal{S}^{*}(R,r,h,d;\theta)\ll\mathcal{L}^{c}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathfrak{I}(Rh)\\
(l,h)=1\end{subarray}}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\theta(l)l^{-1-it}\bigg|\bigg|\sum_{p\simeq P}\bar{\theta}(p)p^{1+it+\beta_{3}}\bigg|\frac{dt}{1+t^{2}}.

For
\sigma=1
, by the large sieve inequality we have

\sum_{R\leq r<2R}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathfrak{I}(Rh)\\
(l,h)=1\end{subarray}}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\theta(l)l^{-s}\bigg|^{2}\ll\tau_{5}(d)^{2}\mathcal{L}^{c}(R^{2}+PRht_{0})(PRht_{0})^{-1}\ll\tau_{5}(d)^{2}\mathcal{L}^{c}

and

\sum_{R\leq r<2R}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}\bigg|\sum_{p\simeq P}\bar{\theta}(p)p^{s}\bigg|^{2}\ll(R^{2}+P)P^{3}.

It follows by Cauchy’s inequality that

\frac{1}{R^{3/2}}\sum_{R\leq r<2R}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\theta(\bmod r)}|\mathcal{S}^{*}(R,r,h,d;\theta)|\ll\tau_{5}(d)h\mathcal{L}^{c}(R^{1/2}P^{3/2}+R^{-1/2}P^{2})\ll\tau_{5}(d)hP^{2}D^{-c}.

This yields (7.15).

Proof of Proposition 7.1: The main term

In this subsection we prove (7.10).

Assume
p\sim P
. We may write

[formula]

\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k}\frac{a_{2}(dk)\mu(k)}{k\varphi(k)}\,\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg).

Since

(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)=\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}=dl}\kappa(m_{1})a_{1}(m_{2})=\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}m_{2}=dl\\
(m_{1},d)=d_{1}\end{subarray}}\kappa(m_{1})a_{1}(m_{2}),

it follows, by substituting
m_{1}=d_{1}l_{1}
, that

[formula]

(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)=\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=l_{1}l_{2}\\
(l_{1},d_{2})=1\end{subarray}}\kappa(d_{1}l_{1})a_{1}(d_{2}l_{2}).

Hence,

\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa*a_{1})(dl)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{pk}\bigg)=\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\,\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}a_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\,\sum_{(l_{1},kd_{2})=1}\kappa(d_{1}l_{1})\Delta\bigg(\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{pk}\bigg).

Inserting this into (7.16) we obtain

[formula]

\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)=\sum_{d_{1}}\,\sum_{d_{2}}\frac{1}{d_{1}d_{2}}\sum_{k}\frac{a_{2}(d_{1}d_{2}k)\mu(k)}{k\varphi(k)}\,\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}a_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\,\sum_{(l_{1},d_{2}k)=1}\kappa(d_{1}l_{1})\Delta\bigg(\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{pk}\bigg).

The innermost sum is, by the Mellin transform, equal to

[formula]

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(3/2)}\bigg(\sum_{(l,d_{2}k)=1}\frac{\kappa(d_{1}l)}{l^{s}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{pk}{l_{2}}\bigg)^{s}\delta(s)\,ds

(here we have rewritten
l
for
l_{1}
). In view of (7.2), we can assume that

d_{2}l_{2}<PT^{-2},\quad d_{1}d_{2}k<PT^{-2}.

Every
l
with
(l,d_{2}k)=1
can be uniquely written as
l=hr
such that
h\in\mathcal{N}(d_{1})
,
(h,d_{2}k)=1
)and
(r,d_{1}d_{2}k)=1
. Hence,

\sum_{(l,kd_{2})=1}\frac{\kappa(d_{1}l)}{l^{s}}=\tilde{\kappa}(d_{1};d_{2}k,s)\lambda(d_{1}d_{2}k,s)\frac{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})\zeta(s+\beta_{2})\zeta(s+\beta_{3})}{\zeta(s)},

By the simple bounds

|\tilde{\kappa}(d_{1};d_{2}k,s)|\leq\tau_{5}(d_{1})\prod_{q|d_{1}}\bigg|1+\frac{c}{q^{\sigma}}\bigg|,\quad|\lambda(m,s)|\leq\prod_{q|m}\bigg|1+\frac{c}{q^{\sigma}}\bigg|

for
\sigma>9/10
, we can move the contour of integration in (7.19) to the vertical segments

\displaystyle s=1+\alpha+it\qquad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad|t|\geq D,

\displaystyle s=1-\mathcal{L}^{-1}+it\quad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad|t|\leq D

and to the two connecting horizontal segments

s=\sigma\pm iD\quad\text{with}\quad 1-\mathcal{L}^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq 1+\alpha.

This yields, by Lemma 5.2 (i) and standard estimates, that the integral (7.18) is equal to the sum of the residues of the integrand at
s=1-\beta_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 3
, plus an acceptable error. Namely we have

\sum_{(l,kd_{2})=1}\kappa(d_{1}l)\Delta\bigg(\frac{ll_{2}}{pk}\bigg)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq 3}\mathcal{R}_{j}\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1};d_{2}k)\lambda_{0j}(d_{1}d_{2}k)\bigg(\frac{pk}{l_{2}}\bigg)^{1-\beta_{j}}+O\bigg(\frac{pk\varepsilon_{1}}{l_{2}}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{R}_{j}
is the residue of the function

\frac{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})\zeta(s+\beta_{2})\zeta(s+\beta_{3})}{\zeta(s)}\delta(s)

at
s=1-\beta_{j}
, and where

\varepsilon_{1}=\exp\{-c\mathcal{L}^{1/10}\}.

This yields

\displaystyle\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}

\displaystyle a_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\,\sum_{(l_{1},kd_{2})=1}\kappa(d_{1}l_{1})\Delta\bigg(\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{pk}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq 3}\mathcal{R}_{j}(pk)^{1-\beta_{j}}\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1};d_{2}k)\lambda_{0j}(d_{1}d_{2}k)\sum_{(l,k)=1}\frac{a_{1}(d_{2}l)}{l^{1-\beta_{j}}}+O(Pk\varepsilon_{1}).

(here we have rewritten
l
for
l_{2}
). Inserting this into (7.18) and rearranging the terms we obtain

[formula]

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{11}(p)=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq 3}\mathcal{R}_{j}p^{1-\beta_{j}}S_{j}^{*}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+O(P\varepsilon_{1})

where

S_{j}^{*}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=\sum_{d}\,\sum_{d_{1}}\,\sum_{k}\frac{a_{2}(d_{1}dk)\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1},dk)\lambda_{0j}(d_{1}dk)\mu(k)}{d_{1}d\varphi(k)k^{\beta_{j}}}\sum_{(l,k)=1}\frac{a_{1}(dl)}{l^{1-\beta_{j}}}.

By the Möbius inversion,

\displaystyle S_{j}^{*}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=

\displaystyle\sum_{d}\sum_{d_{1}}\sum_{k}\frac{a_{2}(d_{1}dk)\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1};dk)\lambda_{0j}(d_{1}dk)\mu(k)}{d_{1}d\varphi(k)k^{\beta_{j}}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{l}\frac{a_{1}(dl)}{l^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg(\sum_{r|(k,l)}\mu(r)\bigg).

This yields, by substituting
k=rk_{1}
,
l=rm
,
n=d_{1}k_{1}
and changing the order of summation,

\displaystyle S_{j}^{*}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=\sum_{r}\,\sum_{d}\frac{|\mu(r)|}{dr\varphi(r)}\sum_{m}\frac{a_{1}(drm)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\sum_{n}\frac{a_{2}(drn)\lambda_{0j}(drn)}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=d_{1}k_{1}\\
(k_{1},r)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1},drk_{1})\mu(k_{1})k_{1}^{1-\beta_{j}}}{\varphi(k_{1})}.

Since

\lambda_{0j}(drn)=\lambda_{0j}(dr)\tilde{\lambda}_{0j}(n,dr),

it follows that

\lambda_{0j}(drn)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=d_{1}k_{1}\\
(k_{1},r)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{0j}(d_{1},drk_{1})\mu(k_{1})k_{1}^{1-\beta_{j}}}{\varphi(k_{1})}=\lambda_{0j}(dr)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r).

Hence

[formula]

S_{j}^{*}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})=S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2}).

On the other hand, by Lemma 5.2 (ii) and direct calculation we have

i\mathcal{R}_{1}p^{-\beta_{1}}=\frac{1}{2\alpha}+O(\mathcal{L}),

i\mathcal{R}_{2}p^{-\beta_{2}}=\frac{2}{\alpha}+O(\mathcal{L}),

i\mathcal{R}_{3}p^{-\beta_{3}}=\frac{3}{2\alpha}+O(\mathcal{L}).

Combining these with (7.20) and (7.21) we obtain (7.10), and complete the proof of Proposition 7.1.
\Box

8. Evaluation of
\Xi_{11}

We first prove a general result as follows.

Lemma 8.1.

For any
\mathbb{a}_{1}
and
\mathbb{a}_{2}
satisfying (7.2),

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\tilde{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-\rho,\bar{\psi})\omega(\rho)=\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+\overline{\Theta_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{2},\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{1})}+o(\mathcal{P}).

Proof.
Write

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s,\psi)=-i\frac{M(s+\beta_{1},\psi)M(s+\beta_{2},\psi)M(s+\beta_{3},\psi)}{M(s,\psi)}.

Assume
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
. By Proposition 2.2, we can choose a rectangle
\mathfrak{R}
with vertices at

s_{0}\pm\alpha+i\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\pm}

such that
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\pm}=\pm\mathcal{L}_{1}+O(\alpha)
and such that the set of zeros of
L(s,\psi)
inside
\mathfrak{R}
is exactly
\tilde{Z}(\psi)
. Further, without loss of generality, we can assume that
|s-\rho|\gg\alpha
if
s\in\mathfrak{R}
and
\rho
is a zero of
L(s,\psi)
. By Lemma 5.9, the residue theorem and a simple bound for
\omega(s)
,

[formula]

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\in\tilde{Z}(\psi)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-\rho,\bar{\psi})\omega(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)-\tilde{I}_{1}^{-}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)+O(\varepsilon)

where

\tilde{I}_{1}^{\pm}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(\pm\alpha)}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds.

Write
s^{\prime}=1-\bar{s}
. If

s=\alpha+s_{0}+iv\in\mathcal{J}(\alpha).

then

s^{\prime}=-\alpha+s_{0}+iv\in\mathcal{J}(-\alpha).

By (2.11) and analytic continuation, for
s\in\mathcal{J}(\alpha)
we have

\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s,\psi)}=-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(s^{\prime},\psi)

and

\overline{A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)}=A(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{2};s^{\prime},\psi)A(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{1},1-s^{\prime},\bar{\psi})\omega(s^{\prime}).

These together imply that

-\tilde{I}_{1}^{-}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)=\overline{\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{2},\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{1};\psi)}.

Hence, by (8.6),

\sum_{\rho\in\tilde{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};\rho,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-\rho,\bar{\psi})\omega(\rho)=\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)+\overline{\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{2},\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{1};\psi)}+O(\varepsilon).

Write

I_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(\alpha)}\mathcal{C}(s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds.

By Lemma 5.2 and 5.9,

\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)-I_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)\ll\mathcal{L}^{-114}\int_{\mathcal{J}(\alpha)}\bigg|L(s+\beta_{2},\psi)L(s+\beta_{3},\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})\omega(s)\,ds\bigg|.

By Cauchy’s inequality, Lemma 6.1, and the second assertion of Lemma 3.3, for
s\in\mathcal{J}(\alpha)
,

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}|L(s+\beta_{2},\psi)L(s+\beta_{3},\psi)|^{2}\ll P^{2}\mathcal{L}^{36},

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}|A(\mathbb{a}_{1};s,\psi)A(\mathbb{a}_{2},1-s,\bar{\psi})|^{2}\ll P^{2}\mathcal{L}^{36}.

These estimates together with (7.4) and (2.9) imply

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\big(\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)-I_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)\big)=o(\mathcal{P}).

On the other hand, moving the segment
\mathcal{J}(\alpha)
to
\mathcal{J}(1)
gives

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}I_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)=\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+O(\varepsilon).

Hence

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2};\psi)=\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{1},\mathbb{a}_{2})+o(\mathcal{P}).

For the sum of
\overline{\tilde{I}_{1}^{+}(\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{2},\bar{\mathbb{a}}_{1};\psi)}
a similar result holds. This completes the proof.
\Box

Recall that
H_{1}(s,\psi)
and
H_{2}(s,\psi)
are given by (2.27). Write

\Xi_{1}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)\big|H_{1}(\rho,\psi)+Z(\rho,\chi\psi)\overline{H_{2}(s,\psi)}\big|^{2}\omega(\rho).

Since
|Z(s,\chi\psi)|=1
if
\sigma=1/2
, it follows that

[formula]

\Xi_{1}=\Xi_{11}+\Xi_{12}+2\Re\{\Xi_{13}\}

where

[formula]

\Xi_{11}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)|H_{1}(\rho,\psi)|^{2}\omega(\rho),

[formula]

\Xi_{12}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)|{H_{2}(\rho,\psi)}|^{2}\omega(\rho),

[formula]

\Xi_{13}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}\mathcal{C}^{*}(\rho,\psi)Z(\rho,\chi\psi)^{-1}H_{1}(\rho,\psi)H_{2}(\rho,\psi)\omega(\rho).

We may write

[formula]

H_{1j}(s,\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{\varkappa_{j}(n)\chi\psi(n)}{n^{s}}

where

\varkappa_{1}(n)=\begin{cases}(1-\log n/\log P_{1})(P_{1}/n)^{\beta_{6}}&\quad\text{if}\quad n<P_{1},\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\varkappa_{2}(n)=\begin{cases}(1-\log n/\log P_{2})(P_{2}/n)^{\beta_{7}}&\quad\text{if}\quad n<P_{2},\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\varkappa_{3}(n)=\begin{cases}(1-\log n/\log P_{3})(P_{3}/n)^{\beta_{6}}&\quad\text{if}\quad n<P_{3},\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In this section we evaluate
\Xi_{11}
. By Lemma 8.1,

[formula]

\Xi_{11}=2\Re\{\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{11},\mathbb{a}_{21})\}+o(\mathcal{P})

with

[formula]

a_{11}(n)=\chi(n)\big(\varkappa_{1}(n)+\iota_{2}\varkappa_{2}(n)\big),\quad a_{21}(n)=\overline{a_{11}(n)}.

By Proposition 7.1, our goal is reduced to evaluating the sum

\displaystyle S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{11},\mathbb{a}_{21})=\sum_{d}\,\sum_{r}

\displaystyle\frac{|\chi(d)||\mu\chi(r)|\lambda_{0j}(dr)}{dr\varphi(r)}\bigg(\sum_{m}\frac{\chi(m)\big(\varkappa_{1}(drm)+\iota_{2}\varkappa_{2}(drm)\big)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{n}\frac{\chi(n)\big(\bar{\varkappa}_{1}(drn)+\bar{\iota}_{2}\bar{\varkappa}_{2}(drn)\big)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)}{n}\bigg).

Write

\beta_{4}=\beta_{1},\quad\beta_{5}=\beta_{2},

so that

\{\beta_{j},\,\beta_{j+1},\,\beta_{j+2}\}=\{\beta_{1},\,\beta_{2},\,\beta_{3}\}.

Lemma 8.2.

Suppose
T<x<P
. Then for
\mu=6,7
,

\sum_{m<x}\frac{\chi(m)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg(\frac{x}{m}\bigg)^{\beta_{\mu}}\log\frac{x}{m}=L^{\prime}(1,\chi)\mathcal{F}_{j\mu}(x)+O(\mathcal{L}^{-6})

where

\mathcal{F}_{j\mu}(x)=\big(1+(\beta_{\mu}-\beta_{j})\log x\big)x^{\beta_{\mu}}.

Proof.
The sum is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}L(1-\beta_{j}+s,\chi)\frac{x^{s}\,ds}{(s-\beta_{\mu})^{2}}.

We move the contour of integration to the vertical segments

\displaystyle s=\alpha+it\qquad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad|t|\geq D,

\displaystyle s=-\mathcal{L}^{-1}+it\quad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad|t|\leq D

and to the two connecting horizontal segments

s=\sigma+\pm iD,\quad\text{with}\quad-\mathcal{L}^{-1}\leq\sigma\leq\alpha.

It follows by Lemma 5.6 that

\displaystyle\sum_{m<x}\frac{\chi(m)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg(\frac{x}{m}\bigg)^{\beta_{\mu}}\log\frac{x}{m}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|s|=5\alpha}L(1-\beta_{j}+s,\chi)\frac{x^{s}\,ds}{(s-\beta_{\mu})^{2}}+O(\varepsilon_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(1,\chi)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{|s|=5\alpha}\frac{x^{s}(s-\beta_{j})}{(s-\beta_{\mu})^{2}}\,ds+O(\mathcal{L}^{-6}).

The result now follows by direct calculation.
\Box

The sum involving
\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)
is more involved. We need the following lemma that will be proved in Appendix A.

Lemma 8.3.

Suppose
dr<PT^{-2}
. The function

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{j}(d,r;s):=\frac{L(s,\chi)}{L(s+\beta_{j+1},\chi)L(s+\beta_{j+2},\chi)}\sum_{n}\frac{\chi(n)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)}{n^{s}}

is analytic and it satisfies

|\mathcal{U}_{j}(d,r;s)|<c\prod_{q|dr}\big(1+cq^{-\sigma}\big)

for
\sigma>9/10
. Further, if
|s-1|\leq 5\alpha
, then

\mathcal{U}_{j}(d,r;s)=\Pi(d,r)+O(\mathcal{L}^{-8})

where

\Pi(d,r)=\prod_{q|dr}\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(q,r)=1\\
q|d\end{subarray}}\frac{1-q^{-1}-\chi(q)q^{-1}}{1-q^{-1}}.

Lemma 8.4.

Suppose
dr<PT^{-2}
and
T<x<P
. Then for
\mu=6,7
,

\sum_{n<x}\frac{\chi(n)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)}{n}\bigg(\frac{x}{n}\bigg)^{-\beta_{\mu}}\log\frac{x}{n}=L^{\prime}(1,\chi)\Pi(d,r)\mathcal{G}_{j\mu}(x)+O(\mathcal{L}^{-6})

where

\mathcal{G}_{j\mu}(x)=\frac{\beta_{j+1}\beta_{j+2}}{\beta_{\mu}^{2}}+\bigg(1-\frac{\beta_{j+1}\beta_{j+2}}{\beta_{\mu}^{2}}-\frac{(\beta_{j+1}-\beta_{\mu})(\beta_{j+2}-\beta_{\mu})}{\beta_{\mu}}\log x\bigg)x^{-\beta_{\mu}}.

Proof.
