\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;s):=\frac{\zeta(s)L(s,\chi)}{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})\zeta(s+\beta_{2})}\sum_{n}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(n,d)\xi_{1}(n;d,l)}{n^{s}}

is analytic and it satisfies

\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;s)\ll\prod_{q|dl}\bigg(1+\frac{c}{q^{9/10}}\bigg)

for
\sigma>9/10
. The right side of (15.14) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(d)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\frac{\zeta(1+s+\beta_{1})\zeta(1+s+\beta_{2})\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;1+s)}{\zeta(1+s)L(1+s,\chi)}\frac{P_{4}^{s+\beta_{3}}}{d^{s}}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(s+\beta_{3})}{s+\beta_{3}}\,ds+O(\varepsilon).

Assume
dl<PT^{-2}
, and
(dl,D)=1
. Note that
P_{4}/d>T
. In a way similar to the proof of Lemma 8.4, we deduce that

[formula]

\mathcal{D}_{1}(d,l)=\lambda_{1}(d)\sum_{j\leq 3}\mathcal{R}_{1j}d^{\beta_{j}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;1-\beta_{j})+O(\varepsilon_{1})

where
\mathcal{R}_{1j}
,
1\leq j\leq 3
, is the residue of the function

[formula]

\frac{\zeta(1+s+\beta_{1})\zeta(1+s+\beta_{2})}{\zeta(1+s)L(1+s,\chi)}\,\frac{P_{4}^{s+\beta_{3}}\omega_{1}(s+\beta_{3})}{s+\beta_{3}}

at
s=-\beta_{j}
(the function (15.16) also has a simple pole at
s=\tilde{\rho}-1
, while the residue at this point can be regarded as an acceptable error). Inserting this into (15.11) and substituting
n=dl
we obtain

[formula]

\Phi_{1}(p)=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{*}Dp}{\varphi(D)}\sum_{j\leq 3}\mathcal{R}_{1j}\mathcal{S}_{1j}+o(p)

where

\mathcal{S}_{1j}=\sum_{n}\frac{b(n)}{n}\sum_{n=dl}\lambda_{1}(d)d^{\beta_{j}}\chi(l)\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;1-\beta_{j}).

If
(q,dl)=1
, then
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q,d)=\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q,1)
and
\xi_{1}(q^{r};d,l)=\xi_{1}(q^{r};1,1)
for any
r
. Hence

[formula]

\frac{\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;s)}{\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;s)}=\prod_{q|dl}\bigg(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q,d)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{1}(q^{r};d,l)}{q^{rs}}\bigg)\bigg(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q,1)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{1}(q^{r};1,1)}{q^{rs}}\bigg)^{-1}.

We can rewrite

[formula]

\mathcal{S}_{1j}=\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;1-\beta_{j})\sum_{n}\frac{b(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}

with

\varpi_{1j}(n)=\sum_{n=dl}\lambda_{1}(d)d^{\beta_{j}}\chi(l)\frac{\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;1-\beta_{j})}{\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;1-\beta_{j})}.

In view of (15.18), we see that
\varpi_{1j}(n)
is a multiplicative function.

Let

\mathcal{Q}=\prod_{q<D^{4}}q.

Every
n
can be uniquely written as
n=n_{1}n_{2}
with
n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})
and
(n_{2},\mathcal{Q})=1
. Hence

[formula]

\sum_{n}\frac{b(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}=\sum_{n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})}\,\frac{\varpi_{1j}(n_{1})}{n_{1}}\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(n_{1}n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}.

Using the the Rankin trick (see [14, Section 13.2], for example), we can impose the constraint
n_{1}<T
to the right side with an acceptable error
O(\varepsilon_{1})
. In addition, by (15.18), for
n<PT^{-3}
,
(n,\mathcal{Q})=1
and
dl=n
,

\lambda_{1}(d)\frac{\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;s)}{\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;s)}=1+O(D^{-c}),

so that

[formula]

\varpi_{1j}(n)=\chi(n)\varrho_{j}^{*}(n)+O(\tau_{2}(n)D^{-c})

where

\varrho_{j}^{*}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d^{\beta_{j}}\chi(d).

The following lemma will be proved in Appendix B.

Lemma 15.1.

Suppose
n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})
and
n_{1}<T
. Then

\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(n_{1}n)\chi(n)\varrho_{j}^{*}(n)}{n}=\chi(n_{1})\tau_{2}(n_{1})\mathfrak{e}_{j}+O\big(\alpha_{1}\tau_{2}(n_{1})\big)

where

\mathfrak{e}_{j}=(e_{1j}+\iota_{2}e_{2j})(\bar{\iota}_{3}e_{3j}+\bar{\iota}_{4}e_{2j})

with

e_{2j}=\bigg(1-\frac{2j}{5}+\frac{8j}{25\pi i}\bigg)\exp\{5\pi i/4\}-\frac{8j}{25\pi i},

e_{3j}=\bigg(1-\frac{2j}{3}+\frac{j}{1.1205\pi i}\bigg)\exp\{0.747\pi i\}-\frac{j}{1.1205\pi i}

and

e_{1j}=e_{1j}^{\prime}-e_{1j}^{\prime\prime},

e_{1j}^{\prime}=\bigg(1-\frac{2j}{3}+\frac{j}{1.134\pi i}\bigg)\exp\{0.756\pi i\}-\frac{j}{1.134\pi i},

e_{1j}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{j}{0.756}\int_{0}^{0.004}\big(\exp\{(3/2)(0.504-z)\pi i\}-\exp\{(3/4)\pi i\}\big)\,dz.

By (15.19)-(15.21) and Lemma 15.1 we obtain

[formula]

\mathcal{S}_{1j}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;1-\beta_{j})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})\\
n<T\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}+O(1/\mathcal{L}).

This remains valid if the constraint
n\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})
on the right side is removed, since, for
D^{4}<q<T
,

\varrho_{j}^{*}(q)=\varrho_{j}(q)+O(\nu(q))\ll\alpha_{1}+\nu(q).

This yields, by (15.21),

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\notin\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})\\
n<T\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}\ll 1/\mathcal{L}.

To apply (15.22) we need two lemmas which will be proved in Appendix A.

Lemma 15.2.

If
|s-1|<5\alpha
, then

\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;s)=\prod_{(q,D)=1}\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-2}}{1-q^{-2}}+O(\alpha_{1}).

Lemma 15.3.

For
\sigma\geq 9/10
the function

\mathcal{U}_{1j}(s):=\frac{1}{\zeta(s)^{2}L(s,\chi)^{2}}\sum_{n}\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n^{s}}

is analytic and bounded. Further we have

\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1)=\frac{\varphi(D)^{2}}{D^{2}}\prod_{(q,D)=1}\frac{(1-q^{-2})^{2}}{1-\chi(q)q^{-2}}+O(\alpha_{1}).

By (4.2) and (4.3),

\displaystyle\sum_{n<T}\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}g\bigg(\frac{T}{n}\bigg)+O(\alpha_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\zeta(1+s)^{2}L(1+s,\chi)^{2}\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1+s)\frac{T^{s}\omega_{1}(s)\,ds}{s}+O(\alpha_{1}).

By Lemma 15.3, we can move the contour of integration in the same way as in the proof of Lemma 8.4 to obtain

\sum_{n<T}\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}=L^{\prime}(1,\chi)^{2}\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1)+O(\alpha_{1}).

This together with Lemma 15.2 and 15.3 yields

\mathcal{M}_{1}(1,1;1-\beta_{j})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})\\
n<T\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\chi(n)\tau_{2}(n)\varpi_{1j}(n)}{n}=\mathfrak{a}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{3}),

since

\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}\prod_{(q,D)=1}(1-q^{-2})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\prod_{q|D}\frac{q}{q+1}.

It follows by (15.22) that

[formula]

\mathcal{S}_{1j}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\mathfrak{a}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{3})

By Lemma 5.8,

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{*}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{24})

and

\mathcal{R}_{1j}=\frac{P_{4}^{\beta_{3}-\beta_{j}}}{(\beta_{j+1}-\beta_{j})(\beta_{j+2}-\beta_{j})L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{3}).

Hence, by direct calculation,

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{11}=1+O(1/\mathcal{L}),

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{12}=2+O(1/\mathcal{L}),

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{13}=1+O(1/\mathcal{L}).

Combining these relations with (15.23) , (15.17) and (15.6) we conclude

[formula]

\Phi_{1}=\big(\mathfrak{e}_{1}+2\mathfrak{e}_{2}+\mathfrak{e}_{3}\big)\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}+o(\mathcal{P})

16. Evaluation of
\Phi_{2}

Recall that
\Phi_{2}
is given by (13.9). Write

\mathcal{K}_{2}(s,\psi)=Z(s,\chi\psi)^{-1}\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}B(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)K(1-s-\beta_{2},\bar{\psi}).

Similar to (15.3),

\Phi_{2}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{1}}\big(I_{3}^{+}(\psi)-I_{3}^{-}(\psi)\big)+O(\varepsilon)

where

I_{3}^{\pm}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(\pm\alpha)}\mathcal{K}_{2}(s,\psi)\omega(s)\,ds.

Note that the length of the sum
B(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)
is
\leq PT^{-1}
. For
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
, moving the segment
\mathcal{J}(-\alpha)
to

\mathcal{J}(-\mathcal{L}^{9})
we obtain, by simple estimation,

I_{3}^{-}(\psi)\ll\varepsilon.

Hence

\Phi_{2}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{1}}I_{3}^{+}(\psi)+O(\varepsilon).

In a way similar to the proof of (15.6), we can move the segment
\mathcal{J}(\alpha)
to
\mathcal{J}(1)
and then extend the sum over
\Psi_{1}
to that over
\Psi
with an acceptable error. Hence

[formula]

\Phi_{2}=\sum_{p\sim P}(pt_{0})^{\beta_{1}}\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi(\bmod\,p)}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}\mathcal{K}_{2}(s,\psi)\omega(s)\,ds+o(\mathcal{P}).

Let
\kappa_{2}(n)
and
b_{1}(n)
be given by

\sum_{n}\frac{\kappa_{2}(n)}{n^{s}}=\frac{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})}{\zeta(s)},\qquad\sigma>1,

\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}=B(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi).

Note that

K(1-s-\beta_{2},\bar{\psi})=\sum_{n}\frac{\tilde{g}_{2}(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s}}

with

\tilde{g}_{2}(y)=y^{\beta_{2}}g^{*}(P_{4}/y).

We can rewrite (16.1) as

\Phi_{2}=\Theta_{2}(\beta_{1},\mathbb{k}_{2}^{*},\mathbb{a}_{2}^{*})+o(p)

with
\kappa^{*}_{2}=\kappa_{2}*b_{1}
and
a^{*}_{2}=\tilde{g}_{2}
. It follows by Proposition 14.1 that

[formula]

\Phi_{2}=\sum_{p\sim P}(pt_{0})^{\beta_{1}}\Phi_{2}(p)+o(\mathcal{P})

where

[formula]

\Phi_{2}(p)=\frac{1}{\varphi(D)}\sum_{k}\frac{\mu\chi(k)}{k\varphi(k)}\sum_{d}\frac{\tilde{g}_{2}(dk)}{d}\,\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa_{2}*b_{1})(dl)\chi(l)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{Dpk}\bigg).

Similar to (7.17),

\displaystyle\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa_{2}*b_{1})

\displaystyle(dl)\chi(l)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{Dpk}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\,\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}b_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\chi(l_{2})\sum_{(l_{1},d_{2}k)=1}\kappa_{2}(d_{1}l_{1})\chi(l_{1})\Delta\bigg(\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{Dpk}\bigg).

The innermost sum is, by the Mellin transform, equal to

[formula]

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}\bigg(\sum_{(l_{1},d_{2}k)=1}\frac{\kappa_{2}(d_{1}l_{1})\chi(l_{1})}{l_{1}^{s}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{Dpk}{l_{2}}\bigg)^{s}\delta(s)\,ds.

For
\sigma>1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(l_{1},d_{2}k)=1}\frac{\kappa_{2}(d_{1}l_{1})\chi(l_{1})}{l_{1}^{s}}=\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(d_{1};d_{2}k,s)\lambda_{2}(d_{1}d_{2}k,s)\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\chi)}{L(s,\chi)}

where

\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(d_{1};r,s)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\mathcal{N}(d_{1})\\
(h,r)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\kappa_{2}(d_{1}h)\chi(h)}{h^{s}}

and

\lambda_{2}(n,s)=\prod_{q|n}\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-s-\beta_{1}}}{1-\chi(q)q^{-s}}.

For notational simplicity we shall write
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(m;r)
and
\lambda_{2}(n)
for
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(m;r,1)
and
\lambda_{2}(n,1)
respectively. Note that
Dpk/l_{2}>T
if
l_{2}<P_{2}^{2}
. In a way similar to the treatment of (7.19),
we see that the expression (16.4) is equal to the residue of the integrand at
s=\tilde{\rho}
plus an acceptable error. Further, by (5.15), we can replace
\tilde{\rho}
by
1
in the expression for this residue, with an acceptable error. Thus we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(l_{1},d_{2}k)=1}\kappa_{2}(d_{1}l_{1})\chi(l_{1})\Delta\bigg(\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{Dpk}\bigg)=\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}\bigg(\frac{Dpk}{l_{2}}\bigg)\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(d_{1};d_{2}k)\lambda_{2}(d_{1}d_{2}k)+O\bigg(\frac{\alpha^{100}\tau_{2}(d_{1})Dpk}{l_{2}}\bigg)

where

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}=\frac{L(1+\beta_{1},\chi)}{L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}\delta(1).

This yields

\displaystyle\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa_{2}*b_{1})(dl)\chi(l)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{Dpk}\bigg)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}Dpk\sum_{d=d_{1}d_{2}}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(d_{1};d_{2}k)\lambda_{2}(dk)\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}\frac{b_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\chi(l_{2})}{l_{2}}

\displaystyle+O\big(\alpha^{50}\tau_{3}(d)Dpk\big).

Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{d}\frac{\tilde{g}_{2}(dk)}{d}

\displaystyle\sum_{(l,k)=1}(\kappa_{2}*b_{1})(dl)\chi(l)\Delta\bigg(\frac{l}{Dpk}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}Dpk\sum_{d_{1}}\,\sum_{d_{2}}\frac{\tilde{g}_{2}(d_{1}d_{2}k)}{d_{1}d_{2}}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(d_{1};d_{2}k)\lambda_{2}(d_{1}d_{2}k)\sum_{(l_{2},k)=1}\frac{b_{1}(d_{2}l_{2})\chi(l_{2})}{l_{2}}

\displaystyle+O(\alpha^{20}Dpk).

Inserting this into (16.3) and rewriting
d
,
l
and
m
for
d_{2}
,
l_{2}
and
d_{1}
respectively, we obtain

[formula]

\Phi_{2}(p)=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}Dp}{\varphi(D)}\sum_{d}\sum_{l}\frac{b_{1}(dl)\chi(l)}{dl}\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)+o(1)

where

\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)=\sum_{(k,l)=1}\frac{\mu\chi(k)}{\varphi(k)}\sum_{m}\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(m;dk)\lambda_{2}(mdk)\tilde{g}_{2}(mdk)}{m}.

On substituting
n=mk
we can write

[formula]

\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)=\sum_{n}\frac{\lambda_{2}(dn)\tilde{g}_{2}(dn)}{n}\xi_{2}(n;d,l)

with

[formula]

\xi_{2}(n;d,l)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=mk\\
(k,l)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu\chi(k)k}{\varphi(k)}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(m;dk).

It can be verified, for given
d
and
l
, that
\xi_{2}(n;d,l)
is a multiplicative function of
n
, and

\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)=\lambda_{2}(d)\sum_{n}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(n,d)\xi_{2}(n;d,l)\tilde{g}_{2}(dn)}{n}

with

[formula]

\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(n,d)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|n\\
(q,d)=1\end{subarray}}\lambda_{2}(q).

Hence, similar to (15.14),

[formula]

\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)=\lambda_{2}(d)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\bigg(\sum_{n}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(n,d)\xi_{2}(n;d,l)}{n^{1+s}}\bigg)\frac{P_{4}^{s+\beta_{2}}}{d^{s}}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(s+\beta_{2})}{s+\beta_{2}}\,ds+O(\varepsilon).

It can be verified, for
\sigma>9/10
, that the function

\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;s):=\frac{\zeta(s)L(s,\chi)}{\zeta(s+\beta_{1})}\sum_{n}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(n,d)\xi_{2}(n;d,l)}{n^{s}}

is analytic and it satisfies

\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;s)\ll\prod_{q|dl}\bigg(1+\frac{c}{q^{9/10}}\bigg).

Thus we can rewrite
\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)
as

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(d)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(1+s+\beta_{1})\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;1+s)}{\zeta(1+s)L(1+s,\chi)}\frac{P_{4}^{s+\beta_{2}}}{d^{s}}\,\frac{\omega_{1}(s+\beta_{2})}{s+\beta_{2}}\,ds+O(\varepsilon).

Assume
dl<P_{2}^{2}
, and
(dl,D)=1
. Note that
P_{4}/d>T
. In a way similar to the proof of (15.15), we deduce that

[formula]

\mathcal{D}_{2}(d,l)=\lambda_{2}(d)\sum_{j=1,2}\mathcal{R}_{2j}d^{\beta_{j}}\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;1-\beta_{j})+O(\varepsilon_{1})

where
\mathcal{R}_{2j}
,
j=1,2
, is the residue of the function

[formula]

\frac{\zeta(1+s+\beta_{1})}{\zeta(1+s)L(1+s,\chi)}\,\frac{P_{4}^{s+\beta_{2}}\omega_{1}(s+\beta_{2})}{s+\beta_{2}}

at
s=-\beta_{j}
. Inserting this into (16,5) and substituting
n=dl
we obtain

[formula]

\Phi_{2}(p)=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}Dp}{\varphi(D)}\sum_{j=1,2}\mathcal{R}_{2j}\mathcal{S}_{2j}+o(p)

where

\mathcal{S}_{2j}=\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)}{n}\sum_{n=dl}\lambda_{2}(d)d^{\beta_{j}}\chi(l)\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;1-\beta_{j}).

Let
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)
be given by

\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)=\mathcal{M}_{2}(1,1;s)\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)\neq 1,

\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)=2\prod_{q>2}\frac{1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}}}{(1-q^{-s})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s})}\bigg(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(q,1)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{2}(q^{r};1,1)}{q^{rs}}\bigg)\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)=1.

The following result will be proved in Appendix A.

Lemma 16.1.

Suppose
dl<PT^{-2}
and
|s-1|<5\alpha
. Then

\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)=\mathfrak{p}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{8})

where

\mathfrak{p}=\prod_{q}\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}\bigg(1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}\bigg)\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)\neq 1,

and

\mathfrak{p}=2\prod_{q>2}\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}\bigg(1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}\bigg)\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)=1.

As a direct consequence of Lemma 16.1 we have
|\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)|\gg 1
if
|s-1|<5\alpha
. Thus we can write

[formula]

\mathcal{S}_{2j}=\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(1-\beta_{j})\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}

with

\varpi_{2j}(n)=\sum_{n=dl}\lambda_{2}(d)d^{\beta_{j}}\chi(l)\frac{\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;1-\beta_{j})}{\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(1-\beta_{j})}.

Note that
\varpi_{2j}(n)
is multiplicative. In a way similar to the proof of (15.20) we have

\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})\\
n_{1}<T\end{subarray}}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(n_{1})}{n_{1}}\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b_{1}(n_{1}n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}+O(D^{-c}).

Assume
n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})
and
n_{1}<T
. For
(n,\mathcal{Q})=1
we have

b_{1}(n_{1}n)=\sum_{n_{1}=l_{1}m_{1}}\,\sum_{n=lm}(l_{1}l)^{-\beta_{3}}g^{*}\bigg(\frac{T^{2}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)b(m_{1}m).

Hence

\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b_{1}(n_{1}n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\sum_{n_{1}=l_{1}m_{1}}\sum_{(l,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(l)}{l(l_{1}l)^{\beta_{3}}}g^{*}\bigg(\frac{T^{2}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)\sum_{(m,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(m_{1}m)\varpi_{2j}(m)}{m}+O(D^{-c}),

since the terms with
(l,m)>1
above contribute
\ll D^{-c}
. Further, for
(m,\mathcal{Q})=1
and
m<P
we have trivially

\varpi_{2j}(m)=\chi(m)\varrho^{*}_{j}(m)+O\big(\tau_{2}(m)D^{-c}\big).

Hence, for
m_{1}<T
,

\sum_{(m,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(m_{1}m)\varpi_{2j}(m)}{m}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\chi(m_{1})\tau_{2}(m_{1})+O(D^{-c})

by Lemma 15.1. It follows that

[formula]

\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b_{1}(n_{1}n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\sum_{n_{1}=l_{1}m_{1}}\chi(m_{1})\tau_{2}(m_{1})\sum_{(l,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(l)}{l(l_{1}l)^{\beta_{3}}}g^{*}\bigg(\frac{T^{2}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)+O(D^{-c}).

On the other hand, if
1<l<T^{5}
and
(l,\mathcal{Q})=1
, then for any
q|l
,

\varrho_{2j}(l)\ll\alpha_{1}+O(\nu(q)).

Thus the innermost sum in (16.14) is equal to
1+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{7})
. It follows that

\sum_{(n,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b_{1}(n_{1}n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\sum_{m_{1}|n_{1}}\chi(m_{1})\tau_{2}(m_{1})+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4}).

Since
1*\chi\tau_{2}=\nu*\chi
(here
1
denotes the arithmetic function identically equal to
1
), it follows that

[formula]

\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\mathfrak{e}_{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})\\
n_{1}<T\end{subarray}}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(n_{1})(\nu*\chi)(n_{1})}{n_{1}}+O(1/\mathcal{L}).

On the right side above, the constraint
n_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{Q})
can be removed with an acceptable error.

The following lemma will be proved in Appendix A.

Lemma 16.2.

The function

\mathcal{U}_{2j}(s)=\frac{1}{\zeta(s)^{3}L(s,\chi)^{3}}\sum_{n}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(n)(\nu*\chi)(n)}{n^{s}}

is analytic and bounded for
\sigma>9/10
. Further we have

\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1)=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D\mathfrak{p}}\prod_{q|D}\frac{q}{q+1}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4}).

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{n<T}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(n)(\nu*\chi)(n)}{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\varpi_{2j}(n)(\nu*\chi)(n)}{n}g\bigg(\frac{T}{n}\bigg)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{10})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\zeta(1+s)^{3}L(1+s,\chi)^{3}\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1+s)T^{s}\omega_{1}(s)\,\frac{ds}{s}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{10}).

The contour of integration is moved in the same way as in the proof of Lemma 8.4. Thus the right side above is, by Lemma 16.2, equal to

L^{\prime}(1,\chi)^{3}\mathcal{U}_{2j}(1)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4})=\frac{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{p}}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4}).

Hence, by (16.15),

\sum_{n}\frac{b_{1}(n)\varpi_{2j}(n)}{n}=\frac{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{e}_{j}}{\mathfrak{p}}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4}).

Inserting this into (16.13) and applying Lemma 16.1 we obtain

[formula]

\mathcal{S}_{2j}=\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{e}_{j}\frac{\varphi(D)}{D}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{4}).

On the other hand, by Lemma 5.8 and direct calculation,

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}=\beta_{1}+O(1/\mathcal{L}^{10}),\quad\mathcal{R}_{2j}=-\frac{1}{\beta_{1}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}+O(\mathcal{L}^{6}),\quad j=1,2,

so that

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{2j}=-\frac{1}{L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}+O(\mathcal{L}^{-1}L^{\prime}(1,\chi)^{-1}).

This together with (16.16) and (16.12) yields

\Phi_{2}(p)=-(\mathfrak{e}_{1}+\mathfrak{e}_{2})\mathfrak{a}p+o(p).

Since
(pt_{0})^{\beta_{1}}=-1+O(\alpha_{1})
, it follows by (16.2) that

[formula]

\Phi_{2}=(\mathfrak{e}_{1}+\mathfrak{e}_{2})\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}+o(\mathcal{P}).

17. Evaluation of
\Phi_{3}

Recall that
\Phi_{3}
is given by (13.10). Write

\mathcal{K}_{3}(s,\psi)=\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}B(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{2},\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)F(1-s,\bar{\psi})

and

I_{4}^{\pm}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(\pm\alpha)}\mathcal{K}_{3}(s,\psi)\omega(s)\,ds.

Similar to the treatment of
\Phi_{2}
, we have

[formula]

\Phi_{3}=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}\big(I_{4}^{+}(\psi)-I_{4}^{-}(\psi)\big)+O(\varepsilon).

To treat the sum of
I_{4}^{+}(\psi)
we move the segment
\mathcal{J}(\alpha)
to
\mathcal{J}(1)
, and then extend the sum over
\Psi_{1}
to the sum over
\Psi
with an acceptable error. Hence

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}I_{4}^{+}(\psi)=\sum_{p\sim P}(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}\Phi_{3}^{+}(p)+o(\mathcal{P})

where

\Phi_{3}^{+}(p)=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi\bmod\,p}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(1)}\mathcal{K}_{3}(s,\psi)\,\omega(s)\,ds.

For
\sigma>1
we can write

\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}B(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{2},\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)=\sum_{m}\frac{\nu^{*}(m)\psi(m)}{m^{s}}.

Thus, replacing the segment
\mathcal{J}(1)
by the line
\sigma=3/2
and integrating term by term give

\Phi_{3}^{+}(p)=\sum_{m}\,\sum_{n<D^{4}}\frac{\nu^{*}(m)\nu(n)}{n}\bigg(\frac{n}{m}\bigg)^{s_{0}}\bigg(\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi\bmod\,p}\psi(m)\bar{\psi}(n)\bigg)\exp\bigg\{-\mathcal{L}^{2}_{2}\log^{2}\frac{n}{m}\bigg\}+O(\varepsilon).

By trivial estimation, the contribution from the terms with
m\neq n
above is
o(p)
. Hence

[formula]

\Phi_{3}^{+}(p)=p\sum_{n<D^{4}}\frac{\nu^{*}(n)\nu(n)}{n}+o(p).

Note that for
n<D^{4}

\nu^{*}(n)=\sum_{n=n_{1}...n_{6}}\kappa_{2}(n_{1})\chi(n_{2}n_{3})\big(\varkappa_{1}(n_{2})+\iota_{2}\varkappa_{2}(n_{2})\big)\big(\bar{\iota}_{3}\varkappa_{3}(n_{3})+\bar{\iota_{4}}\varkappa_{4}(n_{3})\big)\upsilon(n_{4})\tilde{g}_{2}(n_{5})\tilde{g}_{3}(n_{6}).

A detailed analysis shows that (17.2) remains valid if, in the expression for
\nu^{*}(n)
, the factor
\kappa_{2}(n_{1})
is replaced by
(1*\mu)(n_{1})
, the factor

\chi(n_{2}n_{3})\big(\varkappa_{1}(n_{2})+\iota_{2}\varkappa_{2}(n_{2})\big)\big(\bar{\iota}_{3}\varkappa_{3}(n_{3})+\bar{\iota_{4}}\varkappa_{4}(n_{3})\big)

is replaced by
\mathfrak{e}_{0}\chi(n_{2}n_{3})
with

[formula]

\mathfrak{e}_{0}=\big(\varkappa_{1}(1)+\iota_{2}\varkappa_{2}(1)\big)\big(\bar{\iota}_{3}\varkappa_{3}(1)+\bar{\iota_{4}}\varkappa_{4}(1)\big),

and the factors
\tilde{g}_{2}(n_{5})
and
\tilde{g}_{3}(n_{6})
are replaced by
1
respectively. Since

1*\mu*\chi*\chi*\upsilon*1*1=\nu,

it follows that

[formula]

\Phi_{3}^{+}(p)=\mathfrak{e}_{0}p\sum_{n<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}+o(p).

The following lemma will be proved in Appendix B.

Lemma 17.1.

We have

\sum_{n<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}=\mathfrak{a}+o(1)

Since
(pt_{0})^{\beta_{3}}=-1+O(\alpha_{1})
, it follows by (17.5), (17.2) and Lemma 17.1 that

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}I_{3}^{+}(\psi)=-\mathfrak{e}_{0}\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}+o(\mathcal{P}).

To treat the sum involving
I_{4}^{-}(\psi)
on
\mathcal{J}(-\alpha)
we first apply Lemma 5.1 to obtain

\frac{L(s+\beta_{1},\psi)}{L(s,\psi)}=(pt_{0})^{-\beta_{1}}\frac{L(1-s-\beta_{1},\bar{\psi})}{L(1-s,\bar{\psi})}(1+O(\alpha^{6}))

for
\psi\in\Psi_{1}
and
s\in\mathcal{J}(-\alpha)
.
Thus, in a way similar to the proof of (15.4),

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}I_{3}^{-}(\psi)=\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(-\alpha)}\mathcal{K}_{3}^{*}(s,\psi)\omega(s)\,ds+o(\mathcal{P})

where

\mathcal{K}_{3}^{*}(\psi)=\frac{L(1-s-\beta_{1},\bar{\psi})}{L1-s,\bar{\psi})}F(1-s,\bar{\psi})B(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{2},\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi).

Moving the segment
\mathcal{J}(-\alpha)
to
\mathcal{J}(-1)
and then extend the sum over
\Psi_{1}
to then sum over
\Psi
we obtain

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}I_{3}^{-}(\psi)=\sum_{p\sim P}(pt_{0})^{\beta_{2}}\Phi_{3}^{-}(p)+o(\mathcal{P})

where

\Phi_{3}^{-}(p)=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\psi\bmod\,p}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathcal{J}(-1)}\mathcal{K}_{3}^{*}(s,\psi)\,\omega(s)\,ds.

Write

G(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{2},\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)=\sum_{l}\frac{\nu_{1}^{*}(l)\psi(l)}{l^{s}},

so that

B(s,\psi)G(s,\psi)N(s+\beta_{2},\psi)N(s+\beta_{3},\psi)=\sum_{n}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)\psi(n)}{n^{s}}.

On the other hand, for
\sigma<0
,

\frac{L(1-s-\beta_{1},\bar{\psi})}{L(1-s,\bar{\psi})}=\sum_{m}\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m)\bar{\psi}(m)}{m^{1-s}}

with
\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m)=\overline{\kappa_{2}(m)}
, so that

\frac{L(1-s-\beta_{1},\bar{\psi})}{L(1-s,\bar{\psi})}F(1-s,\bar{\psi})=\sum_{n}\frac{\varrho^{*}(n)\bar{\psi}(n)}{n^{1-s}}

with

\varrho^{*}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=lm\\
l<D^{4}\end{subarray}}\nu(l)\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m).

Note that the arithmetic function
(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)
is supported on
n<PT^{-2}
. In a way similar to the proof of (17.3) we deduce that

[formula]

\Phi_{3}^{-}(p)=p\sum_{n}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)\varrho^{*}(n)}{n}+o(p).

We have

\sum_{n}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)\varrho^{*}(n)}{n}=\sum_{l<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(l)}{l}\sum_{m}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(lm)\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m)}{m}.

The inner sum is equal to

\sum_{l=l_{1}l_{2}}\sum_{m_{1}}\,\sum_{(m_{2},l_{1})=1}\frac{b(l_{1}m_{1})\nu_{1}^{*}(l_{2}m_{2})\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m_{1}m_{2})}{m_{1}m_{2}}.

Note that
\nu_{1}^{*}(n)
is supported on
n<T^{5}
. On the right side above, we can drop the terms with
m_{2}>1
or
(m_{1},\mathcal{Q})>1
with an acceptable error. Hence

\sum_{n}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)\varrho^{*}(n)}{n}=\sum_{l<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(l)}{l}\sum_{l=l_{1}l_{2}}\sum_{(m_{1},\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(l_{1}m_{1})\nu_{1}^{*}(l_{2})\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m_{1})}{m_{1}}+o(1).

If
m_{1}
is square-free, then

\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m_{1})=\mu(m_{1})\varrho_{1}(m_{1}).

Hence, by Lemma 15.1 (see (15.)),

\sum_{(m_{1},\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{b(l_{1}m_{1})\bar{\kappa}_{2}(m_{1})}{m_{1}}=\mathfrak{e}_{1}\chi(l_{1})\tau_{2}(l_{1})+O

It follows that

\sum_{n}\frac{(b*\nu_{1}^{*})(n)\varrho^{*}(n)}{n}=\mathfrak{e}_{1}\sum_{l<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(l)}{l}\sum_{l=l_{1}l_{2}}\chi(l_{1})\tau_{2}(l_{1})\nu_{1}^{*}(l_{2})+o(1).

In a way similar to the proof of (17.5), by Lemma 17.1, we find that the right side is equal to

\mathfrak{e}_{1}\sum_{l<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(l)^{2}}{l}+o(1)=\mathfrak{e}_{1}\mathfrak{a}+o(1).

Inserting this into (17.8) gives

\Phi_{3}^{-}(p)=\mathfrak{e}_{1}\mathfrak{a}p+o(p).

Since
(pt_{0})^{\beta_{2}}=1+O(\alpha_{1})
, it follows by (17.7) that

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}(p_{\psi}t_{0})^{\beta_{3}}I_{3}^{-}(\psi)=\mathfrak{e}_{1}\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}+o(\mathcal{P}).

Finally, from (17.1), (17.6) and (17.9) we conclude

[formula]

\Phi_{3}=-(\mathfrak{e}_{0}+\mathfrak{e}_{1})\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}+o(\mathcal{P}).

18. Proof of Proposition 2.5

By the discussion at the end of Section 2, it suffices to prove (2.32) and (2.33).

Proof of (2.32).

By (12.3), (12,17), (13.7), (15.24), (16.17) and (17.10),

[formula]

\Xi_{13}=\mathfrak{c}_{3}\mathfrak{a}\mathcal{P}

with

\mathfrak{c}_{3}=-i(3\mathfrak{e}_{1}+3\mathfrak{e}_{2}+\mathfrak{e}_{3}+\mathfrak{e}_{0})+e^{*}_{1}+2e^{*}_{2}+\epsilon.

In view of (15.), we can write

\mathfrak{e}_{j}=\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{j}-\mathfrak{e}^{\prime\prime}_{j},\quad 1\leq j\leq 3

with

\mathfrak{e}_{j}^{\prime}=(e_{1j}^{\prime}+\iota_{2}e_{2j})(\bar{\iota}_{3}e_{3j}+\bar{\iota}_{4}e_{2j})

\mathfrak{e}_{j}^{\prime\prime}=e_{1j}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\iota}_{3}e_{3j}+\bar{\iota}_{4}e_{2j}).

By calculation (there is a theoretical interpretation),

i(3\mathfrak{e}^{\prime\prime}_{1}+3\mathfrak{e}^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime\prime}_{3})+e^{*}_{1}=\epsilon.

Hence

\mathfrak{c}_{3}=-i(3\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}+3\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{2}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{3}+\mathfrak{e}_{0})+2e^{*}_{2}+2\epsilon.

Direct calculation shows that

[formula]

\Re\{\mathfrak{c}_{3}\}<-6.9951

It follows from (8.24), (9.8) and (18.2) that

\mathfrak{c}_{1}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}+2\Re\{\mathfrak{c}_{3}\}<0.001.

This with together (8.23), (9.7) and (18.1) yields (2.32).

Proof of (2.33).

By Lemma 8.1,

[formula]

\sum_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\,\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{Z}(\psi)}|J_{1}(\rho,\psi)|^{2}\omega(\rho)=2\Re\{\Theta_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23}\}+o(\mathcal{P}).

We have

\displaystyle S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})=

\displaystyle\sum_{r}\,\sum_{d}\frac{|\chi(d)||\mu\chi(r)|\lambda_{0j}(dr)}{dr\varphi(r)}\bigg(\sum_{m}\frac{\chi(m)\tilde{f}\big(\log(drm)/\log P\big)}{m^{1-\beta_{j}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{n}\frac{\chi(n)\tilde{f}\big(\log(drn)/\log P\big)\xi_{0j}(n;d,r)}{n}\bigg).

The right side is split into three sums according to

dr<P^{0.5},\quad P^{0.5}\leq dr<P^{0.502},\quad P^{0.502}\leq dr<P^{0.504}.

By the discussion in Section 8 and 10, the sum over
dr<P^{0.5}
contributes
o(\alpha)
; the sum over
P^{0.5}\leq dr<P^{0.502}

is equal to

\bigg(\frac{500L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}{\log P}\bigg)^{2}\sum_{P^{0.5}\leq n<P^{0.502}}\frac{|\chi(n)|\lambda_{0j}(n)}{\varphi(n)}\big(-1-\beta_{j}\log(n/P^{0.5})\big)\big(-1+\mathcal{Y}_{1j}(n)\big)+o(\alpha);

the sum over
P^{0.502}\leq dr<P^{0.504}
is equal to

\bigg(\frac{500L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}{\log P}\bigg)^{2}\sum_{P^{0.502}\leq n<P^{0.504}}\frac{|\chi(n)|\lambda_{0j}(n)}{\varphi(n)}\big(1-\beta_{j}\log(P^{0.504}/n)\big)\big(1+\mathcal{Y}_{1j}(n)\big)+o(\alpha).

The factors
\beta_{j}\log(n/P^{0.5})
,
\beta_{j}\log(P^{0.504}/n)
and
\mathcal{Y}_{1j}(n)
in the above sums make minor contribution. If we disregard these factors, the major contribution to
S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})
will be

\bigg(\frac{500L^{\prime}(1,\chi)}{\log P}\bigg)^{2}\sum_{P^{0.5}\leq n<P^{0.504}}\frac{|\chi(n)|\lambda_{0j}(n)}{\varphi(n)}=\frac{1000\mathfrak{a}}{\log P}+o(\alpha).

Thus we have the crude bound

\Re\{S_{j}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})\}<\frac{1100\mathfrak{a}}{\log P},

so that

\Re\bigg\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}S_{1}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})+\frac{2}{\alpha}S_{2}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})+\frac{3}{2\alpha}S_{3}(\mathbb{a}_{23},\mathbb{a}_{23})\bigg\}<\frac{4400\mathfrak{a}}{\pi}.

This yields (2.33) by Lemma 8.1 and Proposition 7.1.
\Box

Appendix A. Some Euler Products

This appendix is devoted to proving Lemma 8.3, 15.2, 15.3, 16.1 and 16.2. For notational simplicity we shall write

u=q^{-1},\qquad v=\chi(q).

Proof of Lemma 8.3.
Note that

\lambda(km,s)=\lambda(k,s)\tilde{\lambda}(m,k;s)

with

\tilde{\lambda}(m,k;s)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|m\\
(q,k)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{(1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{2}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{3}})}{1-q^{-s}}.

For given
d
and
h
, the function
\tilde{\lambda}(m,h,d;1-\beta_{j})\xi_{j}(m;d,h)
is multiplicative in
m
, and
\tilde{\lambda}(q^{r},k;s)=\tilde{\lambda}(q,k;s)
for any
r
. Thus, for
\sigma>1
,

\mathcal{U}_{j}(d,h;s)=\lambda(dh,1-\beta_{j})\prod_{q}\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,1,s;q)

with

\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=\frac{(1-\chi(q)q^{-s-\beta_{j+1}})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s-\beta_{j+2}})}{1-\chi(q)q^{-s}}\bigg(1+\tilde{\lambda}(q,dh;1-\beta_{j})\sum_{r}\frac{\chi(q^{r})\xi_{j}(q^{r};d,h)}{q^{rs}}\bigg).

Since

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}(q;d,1-\beta_{j})\chi(q)\xi_{j}(q^{r};d,h)

\displaystyle=\chi(q)(\kappa(q)-q^{-\beta_{j}})+O(q^{-1})

\displaystyle=\chi(q)(q^{-\beta_{j+1}}+q^{-\beta_{j+2}}-1)+O(q^{-1}),

we have
\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=1+O(q^{-9/5})
for
\sigma>9/10
, so
\mathcal{U}_{j\mu}(d,s)
is analytic in this region, and

\mathcal{U}_{j}(d,h;s)=\lambda(dh,1-\beta_{j})\prod_{q<D}\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h_{1},s;q)+O(D^{-c}).

Since

\lambda(dh,1-\beta_{j})=\frac{\varphi(dh)^{2}}{(dh_{1})^{2}}+O(\alpha_{1})

and
\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=1
if
q|D
, the proof is reduced to showing that

[formula]

\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=1+O(\alpha\log q/q)\quad\text{if}\quad(q,dh_{1})=1,

[formula]

\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=\frac{1}{1-vu}+O(\alpha\log q/q)\quad\text{if}\quad q|h_{1},

and

[formula]

\mathcal{T}_{j}(d,h,s;q)=\frac{1-u-vu}{(1-vu)(1-u)}+O(\alpha\log q/q)\quad\text{if}\quad q|d\quad(q,h_{1})=1,

provided

|s-1|<5\alpha,\quad q<D\quad\text{and}\quad(q,D)=1,

which are henceforth assumed. We discuss in three cases.

Case 1.

(q,dh)=1
.

We have

\xi_{j}(q^{r},dh)=\tilde{\kappa}(q^{r};dh,1-\beta_{j})-\frac{\kappa(q^{r-1})q^{1-\beta_{j}}}{q-1}.

because
\tilde{\kappa}(q^{r-1};dq,1-\beta_{j})=\kappa(q^{r-1})
.
Note that

\kappa(q^{r})=\sum_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}=r}q^{\mu_{1}\beta_{1}+\mu_{2}\beta_{2}+\mu_{3}\beta_{3}}-\sum_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}=r-1}q^{\mu_{1}\beta_{1}+\mu_{2}\beta_{2}+\mu_{3}\beta_{3}}=\tau_{2}(q^{r})(1+O(\alpha r\log q))

where
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{3}
run through non-negative integers. Hence

\xi_{j}(q^{r},dh)=\sum_{\eta=0}^{\infty}(r+\eta+1)u^{\eta}-\frac{r}{1-u}+O(\alpha r\log q/q)=\frac{1}{(1-u)^{2}}+O(\alpha r\log q/q).

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{r}\frac{\chi(q^{r})\xi_{j}(q^{r};d,h)}{q^{rs}}=\frac{1}{(1-u)^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{1-vu}-1\bigg)+O(\alpha\log q/q).

This together with the relations

\tilde{\lambda}(q,dh;1-\beta_{j})=(1-u)^{2}+O(\alpha\log q/q)

and

\frac{(1-\chi(q)q^{-s-\beta_{j+1}})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s-\beta_{j+2}})}{1-\chi(q)q^{-s}}=1-vu+O(\alpha\log q/q),

yields (A.1).

Case 2.

q|h
.

We have

\xi_{j}(q^{r},dh)=\kappa(q^{r})=r+1+O(\alpha r\log q/q),

so that

\sum_{r}\frac{\chi(q^{r})\xi_{j}(q^{r};d,h)}{q^{rs}}=\frac{1}{(1-vu)^{2}}-1+O(\alpha\log q/q).

This yields (A.2) since
\tilde{\lambda}(q,dh;1-\beta_{j})=1
.

Case 3.

q|d
,
(q,h_{1})=1
.

We also have
\tilde{\lambda}(q,dh;1-\beta_{j})=1
and

\xi_{j}(q^{r},dh)=\kappa(q^{r})-\frac{\kappa(q^{r-1})q}{q-1}=r+1-\frac{r}{1-u}+O(\alpha r\log q/q),

so that

\sum_{r}\frac{\chi(q^{r})\xi_{j}(q^{r};d,h)}{q^{rs}}=\frac{1}{(1-vu)^{2}}-1-\frac{vu}{(1-u)(1-vu)^{2}}+O(\alpha\log q/q).

This yields (A.3).
\Box

To prove Lemma 15.2 and 15.3 we need several prerequisite results.
In what follows assume
dl<P_{2}^{2}
,
(dl,D)=1
and
|s-1|<5\alpha
. We have

[formula]

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(d,l;s)=

\displaystyle\prod_{q<D}\frac{(1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{2}})}{(1-q^{-s})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s})}\bigg(1+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q,d)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{1}(q^{r};d,l)}{q^{rs}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\big(1+O(D^{-c})\big).

Note that

\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r-1},dq)=\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1}),

so that

[formula]

\xi_{1}(q^{r};d,l)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},d)-\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1})&\quad\text{if}\quad(q,l)=1\\
\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},d)&\quad\text{if}\quad q|l\end{cases}

for any
q
,
d
,
l
and
r
.

Proof of Lemma 15.2.
By (A.4), (A.5) and the relations

\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(q^{r},1)=\lambda_{1}(q),\quad\frac{(1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}})(1-q^{-s-\beta_{2}})}{(1-q^{-s})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s})}=\frac{1-q^{-1}}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}+O\bigg(\frac{\alpha\log q}{q}\bigg),

it suffices to show that

[formula]

\displaystyle 1+\lambda_{1}(q)\sum_{r}\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\bigg(\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},1)-\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1})\bigg)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}{1-q^{-1}}\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-2}}{1-q^{-2}}

\displaystyle+O\bigg(\frac{\alpha\log q}{q}\bigg)

if
q<D
,
(q,D)=1
, and

[formula]

\displaystyle 1+\lambda_{1}(q)\sum_{r}\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\bigg(\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},1)-\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1})\bigg)=\frac{1}{1-q^{-1}}+O\bigg(\frac{\alpha\log q}{q}\bigg)

if
q|D
.

Assume
q<D
. Since

\kappa_{1}(q^{r})=\sum_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=r}q^{-\mu_{1}\beta_{1}-\mu_{2}\beta_{2}}-\sum_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=r-1}q^{-\mu_{1}\beta_{1}-\mu_{2}\beta_{2}},

where
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
run through non-negative integers, it follows that

\kappa_{1}(q^{r})=1+O(\alpha r\log q)

if
q^{r}<P
. Hence

\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},1)=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{N}(q)}\frac{\kappa_{1}(q^{r}h)\chi(h)}{h}=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{N}(q)}\frac{\chi(h)}{h}+O(\alpha r\log q)=\frac{1}{1-vu}+O(\alpha r\log q).

It follows that

\sum_{r}\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\bigg(\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},1)--\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1})\bigg)=\frac{u}{1-u}\bigg(\frac{1}{1-vu}-\frac{v}{1-u}\bigg)+O(\alpha\log q/q).

This together with the relation

\lambda_{1}(q)=1-vu+O(\alpha\log q/q)

yields

1+\lambda_{1}(q)\sum_{r}\frac{1}{q^{rs}}\bigg(\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(q^{r},1)-\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{1}(q^{r-1})\bigg)=\frac{1}{1-u}-\frac{uv(1-vu)}{(1-u)^{2}}+O(\alpha\log q/q).

It is direct to verify that

\frac{1}{1-u}-\frac{uv(1-vu)}{(1-u)^{2}}=\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}{1-q^{-1}}\frac{1-\chi(q)q^{-2}}{1-q^{-2}}

if
\chi(q)=\pm 1
, and

\frac{1}{1-u}-\frac{uv(1-vu)}{(1-u)^{2}}=\frac{1}{1-q^{-1}}

if
\chi(q)=0
, whence (A.6) and (A.7) follow.
\Box

Proof of Lemma 15.3.
We give a sketch only, as the situation is similar to Lemma 15.2. it can be verified that the factor

\mathcal{U}_{1j}(q,s)
in the Euler product representation

\mathcal{U}_{1j}(s)=\prod_{q}\mathcal{U}_{1j}(q,s)

satisfies
\mathcal{U}_{1j}(q,s)=1+O(q^{-2\sigma})
if
(q,D)=1
. Further, in the case
q\leq D
we have

\mathcal{U}_{1j}(q,1)=(1-q^{-1})^{2}+O(\alpha_{1}/q)\quad\text{if}\quad q|D,

and

\mathcal{U}_{1j}(q,1)=\frac{(1-q^{-2})^{2}}{1-\chi(q)q^{-2}}+O(\alpha_{1}/q)\quad\text{if}\quad(q,D)=1.

This completes the proof.
\Box

Proof of Lemma 16.1.
For any
q
,
r
,
d
and
l
we have

\xi_{2}(q^{r};d,l)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(q^{r};d)-\frac{\chi(q)q}{q-1}\kappa_{2}(q^{r-1})&\quad\text{if}\quad(q,l)=1\\
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(q^{r};d)&\quad\text{if}\quad q|l,\end{cases}

|\kappa_{2}(q^{r})|=|q^{-\beta_{1}}-1|\ll\alpha\log q,\qquad\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(q,d)=1+O(\alpha\log q/q).

Hence

1+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(q,d)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{2}(q^{r};d,l)}{q^{rs}}=1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}+O(\alpha\log q/q)\quad\text{if}\quad(q,l)=1

and

1+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}(q,d)\sum_{r}\frac{\xi_{2}(q^{r};d,l)}{q^{rs}}=1+O(\alpha\log q/q)\quad\text{if}\quad q|l.

On the other hand we have

\frac{1-q^{-s-\beta_{1}}}{(1-q^{-s})(1-\chi(q)q^{-s})}=\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}+O(\alpha\log q/q).

It follows that

\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;s)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q<D\\
(q,l)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}\bigg(1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}\bigg)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q<D\\
q|l\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-\chi(q)q^{-1}}+O(\alpha_{1}).

It is direct to verify that in either case the assertion holds.
\Box

Proof of Lemma 16.2.
We give a sketch only. If
dl<D
,
(dl,D)=1
and
|s-1|\leq 5\alpha
, then

\frac{\mathcal{M}_{2}(d,l;s)}{\mathcal{M}_{2}^{*}(s)}=\Pi_{2}(l)+O(\alpha_{1}\tau_{2}(dl))

with

\Pi_{2}(l)=\prod_{q|l}\bigg(1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}\bigg)^{-1}\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)\neq 1,

\Pi_{2}(l)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|l\\
q>2\end{subarray}}\bigg(1-\frac{\chi(q)}{q-1}\bigg)^{-1}\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)=1,\,\,2|l,

\Pi_{2}(l)=0\quad\text{if}\quad\chi(2)=(l,2)=1.

The assertion follows by discussing the cases
\chi(2)\neq 1
and
\chi(2)=1
respectively.
\Box

Appendix B. Some arithmetic sums

Proof of Lemma 15.1.
Put

\varrho_{j}(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)d^{\beta_{j}}.

First we claim that

[formula]

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<P\\
(n,\mathcal{Q})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{|\varrho_{j}(n)-\varrho^{*}_{j}(n)|}{n}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-8}.

Since
\chi=\mu*\nu
, it follows that

\chi(d)=\mu(d)+O\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h|d\\
h>1\end{subarray}}\nu(h)\bigg).

Hence

\varrho_{j}(n)-\varrho^{*}_{j}(n)\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h|n\\
h>1\end{subarray}}\nu(h)\tau_{2}(n/h).

If
h>1
and
(h,\mathcal{Q})=1
, then
h>D^{4}
. Hence, by substituting
n=hm
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<P\\
(n,\mathcal{Q})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{|\varrho_{j}(n)-\varrho^{*}_{j}(n)|}{n}\ll\sum_{h>D^{4}}\frac{\nu(h)}{h}\sum_{m<P/h}\frac{\tau_{2}(m)}{m}\ll(\log P)^{2}\sum_{D^{4}<h<P}\frac{\nu(h)}{h}.

This together with Lemma 3.2 yields (B.1).

Next we claim that

[formula]

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<P\\
(n,\mathcal{Q})>1\end{subarray}}\frac{|\varrho_{j}(n)|}{n}\ll\mathcal{L}^{-8}.

Note that
\varrho_{j}(q^{r})=\varrho_{j}(q)
for any
r
, and
\varrho_{j}(q)\ll\alpha\log q
if
q<P
. Thus the left side above is

\leq\bigg(\sum_{q<D^{4}}\frac{|\varrho_{j}(q)|}{q}\bigg)\prod_{q<P}\big(1+|\varrho_{j}(q)|/q+O(1/q^{2})\big)\ll\alpha\sum_{q<D^{4}}\frac{\log q}{q}.

This yields (B.2).

By (B.1) and (B.2), for
\mu=2,3
,

\sum_{(l,\mathcal{Q})=1}\frac{\varkappa_{\mu}(l_{1}l)\varrho^{*}_{j}(l)}{l}=\sum_{l}\frac{\varkappa_{\mu}(l_{1}l)\varrho_{j}(l)}{l}+O(\mathcal{L}^{-8}).

We proceed to prove theassertion with
\mu=2
. Since

\sum_{l}\frac{\varrho_{j}(l)}{l^{s}}=\frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta(s-\beta_{j})}

for
\sigma>1
and

\varkappa_{2}(m)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\frac{(P_{2}/m)^{s}}{(\log P_{2})(s-\beta_{7})^{2}}\,ds,

it follows that

\sum_{l}\frac{\varkappa_{2}(l_{1}l)\varrho_{j}(l)}{l}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\frac{\zeta(1+s)}{\zeta(1+s-\beta_{j})}\frac{(P_{2}/l_{1})^{s}}{(\log P_{2})(s-\beta_{7})^{2}}\,ds.

In a way similar to the proof of Lemma 8.1, we see that the right side is equal to the sum of the residues of the integrand at
s=0
and
s=\beta_{7}
plus an acceptable error. By direct calculation, the residue at
s=0
is

-\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{7}^{2}\log P_{2}}=-\frac{8j}{25\pi i}+O(\alpha_{1});

the residue at
s=\beta_{7}
is, by the Cauchy integral formula,

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(1+\beta_{7})}{\zeta(1+\beta_{7}-\beta_{j})}

\displaystyle\frac{\log(P_{2}/l_{1})}{\log P_{2}}\bigg(\frac{P_{2}}{l_{1}}\bigg)^{\beta_{7}}+\frac{1}{\log P_{2}}\bigg(\frac{P_{2}}{l_{1}}\bigg)^{\beta_{7}}\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(1+s)}{\zeta(1+s-\beta_{j})}|_{s=\beta_{7}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\frac{2j}{5}+\frac{8j}{25\pi i}\bigg)\exp\{5\pi i/4\}+O(\alpha_{1}).

These together complete the proof in case
\mu=2
.
In case
\mu=3
the proof can be obtained with
\beta_{6}
and
P_{3}
in place of
\beta_{7}
and
P_{2}
respectively. The same argument also gives

\sum_{l}\frac{\varkappa_{1}(l_{1}l)\varrho_{j}(l)}{l}=\bigg(1-\frac{2j}{3}+\frac{j}{1.134\pi i}\bigg)\exp\{0.756\pi i\}-\frac{j}{1.134\pi i}+O(\alpha_{1}).

For
\mu=1
the proof is therefore reduced to showing that

[formula]

\sum_{l>P^{12}/l_{1}}\frac{\varkappa_{1}(l_{1}l)\varrho_{j}(l)}{l}=\frac{j}{0.756}\int_{0}^{0.004}\big(\exp\{(3/2)(0.504-z)\pi i\}-\exp\{(3/4)\pi i\}\big)\,dz+O(\alpha_{1}).

By (4.2) and (4.3), the left side of (B.3) is equal to

\sum_{l}\frac{\varrho_{j}(l)}{l}\bigg(\frac{P_{1}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)^{\beta_{6}}\frac{1}{0.504}\int_{0.5}^{0.504}\bigg\{g\bigg(\frac{P^{z}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)-g\bigg(\frac{P^{0.5}}{l_{1}l}\bigg)\bigg\}\,dz+O(\alpha_{1}).

By a change of variable, for
0.5\leq z\leq 0.504
,

\bigg(\frac{P_{1}}{y}\bigg)^{\beta_{6}}\bigg\{g\bigg(\frac{P^{z}}{y}\bigg)-g\bigg(\frac{P^{0.5}}{y}\bigg)\bigg\}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\big(P^{\beta_{6}(0.504-z)}P^{zs}-P^{0.004\beta_{6}}P^{0.5s}\big)\frac{\omega_{1}(s-\beta_{6})\,ds}{y^{s}(s-\beta_{6})}.

Hence, in a way similar to the proof of, we find that the left side of (B.3) is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{0.504}

\displaystyle\int_{0.5}^{0.504}\,\bigg\{\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\big(P^{\beta_{6}(0.504-z)}P^{zs}-P^{0.004\beta_{6}}P^{0.5s}\big)\frac{\zeta(1+s)}{\zeta(1+s-\beta_{j})}\frac{\omega_{1}(s-\beta_{6})\,ds}{l_{1}^{s}(s-\beta_{6})}\bigg\}dz+O

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{0.504}\frac{\beta_{j}}{\beta_{6}}\int_{0.5}^{0.504}\big(P^{\beta_{6}(0.504-z)}-P^{0.004\beta_{6}}\big)\,dz+O(\alpha_{1}).\quad\Box

Proof of Lemma 17.1.
By Lemma 3.1,

\sum_{n<D^{4}}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}=\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n}g\bigg(\frac{T}{n}\bigg)+o(1).

The sum on the right side is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(1)}\bigg(\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n^{1+s}}\bigg)\frac{T^{s}\omega_{1}(s)}{s}\,ds.

Assume
\sigma>1
. We have

\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n^{s}}=\prod_{p}\bigg(1+\sum_{r}\frac{\nu(p^{r})^{2}}{p^{rs}}\bigg).

If
\chi(p)=1
, then (see [19, (1.2.10)])

1+\sum_{r}\frac{\nu(p^{r})^{2}}{p^{rs}}=(1-p^{-s})^{-4}(1-p^{-2s})=(1-p^{-s})^{-2}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-2}(1-p^{-2s});

if
\chi(p)=-1
, then

1+\sum_{r}\frac{\nu(p^{r})^{2}}{p^{rs}}=1+\sum_{r}\frac{1}{p^{2rs}}=(1-p^{-2s})^{-1}=(1-p^{-s})^{-2}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-2}(1-p^{-2s});

if
\chi(p)=0
, then

1+\sum_{r}\frac{\nu(p^{r})^{2}}{p^{rs}}=1+\sum_{r}\frac{1}{p^{rs}}=(1-p^{-s})^{-1}=(1-p^{-s})^{-2}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-2}(1-p^{-s}).

Hence

\sum_{n}\frac{\nu(n)^{2}}{n^{s}}=\zeta(s)^{2}L(s,\chi)^{2}\prod_{(p,D)=1}(1-p^{-2s})\prod_{p|D}(1-p^{-s}).

In a way similar to the proof of, by (A) and simple estimate, we find that the integral (14) is equal to the residue of the function

\zeta(1+s)^{2}L(1+s,\chi)^{2}\prod_{(p,D)=1}(1-p^{-2(1+s)})\prod_{p|D}(1-p^{-(1+s)})\frac{T^{s}\omega_{1}(s)}{s}

at
s=0
, plus an acceptable error
O
, which is equal to

L^{\prime}(1,\chi)^{2}\prod_{p}(1-p^{-2})\prod_{p|D}(1-p^{-1})(1-p^{-2})^{-1}+o(1)=\mathfrak{a}+o(1).\quad\Box
